Point Processes and Multiple SLE/GFF Coupling This manuscript was prepared for the lectures given in the 4th ZiF Summer School ‘Randomness in Physics and Mathematics: From Integrable Probability to Disordered Systems’ held at ZiF–Center for Interdisciplinary Research, Bielefeld University, Germany, from 1st to 13th August 2022, which has been organized by Gernot Akemann and Friedrich Götze.
Makoto Katori

Abstract
In the series of lectures, we will discuss probability laws of random points, curves, and surfaces. Starting from a brief review of the notion of martingales, one-dimensional Brownian motion (BM), and the D D -dimensional Bessel processes, BES D , D ≥ 1 D\geq 1 , first we study Dyson’s Brownian motion model with parameter β > 0 \beta>0 , DYS β , which is a one-parameter family of repulsively interacting N N Brownian motions on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} , N ∈ ℕ := { 1 , 2 , … } N\in{\mathbb{N}}:=\{1,2,\dots\} , and is regarded as multivariate extensions of BES D with the relation β = D − 1 \beta=D-1 . In particular, the determinantal structures are proved for DYS 2 , which is realized as the eigenvalue process of Hermitian-matrix-valued BM studied in random matrix theory. Next, using the reproducing kernels of Hilbert function spaces, the Gaussian analytic functions (GAFs) are defined on a unit disk 𝔻 {\mathbb{D}} and an annulus 𝔸 q := { z ∈ ℂ : q < | z | < 1 } {\mathbb{A}}_{q}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:q<|z|<1\} , q ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) q\in(0,1) , which provide models of random surfaces. As zeros of the GAFs, determinantal point processes and permanental-determinantal point processes are obtained, which have symmetry and invariance associated with conformal transformations. Then, the Schramm–Loewner evolution with parameter κ > 0 \kappa>0 , SLE κ , is introduced, which is driven by a BM on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} and generates a family of conformally invariant probability laws of random curves on the upper half complex plane ℍ {\mathbb{H}} . We regard SLE κ as a complexification of BES D with the relation κ = 4 / ( D − 1 ) \kappa=4/(D-1) . The last topic of lectures is the construction of the multiple SLE κ , which is driven by the N N -particle DYS β on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} and generates N N interacting random curves in ℍ {\mathbb{H}} . There, we define the Gaussian free field (GFF) and its generalization called the imaginary surface with parameter χ \chi , which are considered as the distribution-valued random fields on ℍ {\mathbb{H}} . Under the relation χ = 2 / κ − κ / 2 \chi=2/\sqrt{\kappa}-\kappa/\sqrt{2} , we characterize the SLE/GFF coupling studied by Dubédat, Sheffield, and Miller by its temporal stationarity, and extend it to multiple cases. We prove that the multiple SLE/GFF coupling is established, if and only if the driving N N -particle process on ℝ {\mathbb{R}} is identified with DYS β with the relation β = 8 / κ \beta=8/\kappa . This relation between parameters is very simple, but highly nontrivial, since if we simply combine the relations β = D − 1 \beta=D-1 and κ = 4 / ( D − 1 ) \kappa=4/(D-1) mentioned above, we will have a different relation β = 4 / κ \beta=4/\kappa . Under the present multiple SLE/GFF coupling with β = 8 / κ \beta=8/\kappa , we can prove that the multiple SLE driven by DYS β exhibits the phase transitions at κ = 4 \kappa=4 and κ = 8 \kappa=8 in the similar way to the original SLE κ with a single SLE curve. Keywords Itô’s formula, martingales, conformal transformations, point processes, SLE curves, imaginary surfaces, multiple SLE/GFF coupling



1
Determinantal Martingales and Determinantal Stochastic Processes

1.1
One-dimensional standard Brownian motion (BM)

First of all, we consider the motion of a
Brownian particle in one-dimensional space
\mathbb{R}

starting from the origin 0 at time
t=0
.
At each time
t>0
, the particle position is randomly distributed,
and each realization of its path (trajectory)
is denoted by
\omega
and called a

sample path

or simply a

path
.
Let
\Omega
be the collection of all sample paths
and we call it the

sample path space
.
The position of the Brownian particle at time
t\geq 0

in a path
\omega\in\Omega

is written as
B(t,\omega)
. Usually we omit
\omega

and simply write it as
B(t),\ t\geq 0
.

We represent each event associated with the process by a subset of
\Omega
,
and the collection of all events is denoted by
{\cal F}
.
The whole sample path space
\Omega
and the empty set
\emptyset

are in
{\cal F}
.
For any two sets
{\sf A},{\sf B}\in{\cal F}
, we assume that

{\sf A}\cup{\sf B}\in{\cal F}
and
{\sf A}\cap{\sf B}\in{\cal F}
.
If
{\sf A}\in{\cal F}
, then its complement
{\sf A}^{\rm c}
is also in
{\cal F}
.
It is closed for any infinite sum of events in the sense that,
if
{\sf A}_{n}\in{\cal F},n=1,2,\dots
, then

\cup_{n\geq 1}{\sf A}_{n}\in{\cal F}
.
Such a collection of events
{\cal F}
is said to be a

\sigma
-field
(sigma-field).
The symbol
\sigma
is for ‘sum’.

A
probability measure

{\rm P}
is a nonnegative function
defined on the
\sigma
-field
{\cal F}
.
Since any element of
{\cal F}
is given by a set as above,
any input of
{\rm P}
is a set;
{\rm P}
is a

set function
.
It satisfies the following properties:

{\rm P}[{\sf A}]\geq 0
for all
{\sf A}\in{\cal F}
,

{\rm P}[\Omega]=1
,
{\rm P}[\emptyset]=0
,
and if
{\sf A},{\sf B}\in{\cal F}
are disjoint,
{\sf A}\cap{\sf B}=\emptyset
,
then
{\rm P}[{\sf A}\cup{\sf B}]={\rm P}[{\sf A}]+{\rm P}[{\sf B}]
.
In particular,

{\rm P}[{\sf A}^{\rm c}]=1-{\rm P}[{\sf A}]
for all
{\sf A}\in{\cal F}
.
The triplet
(\Omega,{\cal F},{\rm P})
is called
the
probability space
.

The smallest
\sigma
-field containing all intervals on
\mathbb{R}

is called the
Borel
\sigma
-field
and denoted by
{\cal B}(\mathbb{R})
.
A
random variable
or

measurable function

is a real-valued function
f(\omega)
on
\Omega

such that, for every Borel set
A\in{\cal B}(\mathbb{R})
,

f^{-1}(A)\in{\cal F}
.
Two events
{\sf A}
and
{\sf B}
are said to be

independent

if
{\rm P}[{\sf A}\cap{\sf B}]={\rm P}[{\sf A}]{\rm P}[{\sf B}]
.
Two random variables
X
and
Y
are

independent

if the events
{\sf A}=\{X:X\in A\}
and
{\sf B}=\{Y:Y\in B\}
are independent
for any
A,B\in{\cal B}(\mathbb{R})
.

The

one-dimensional standard Brownian motion
,

\{B(t,\omega):t\geq 0\}
,
has the following three properties:

(BM1)

B(0,\omega)=0
with probability one.

(BM2)
There is a subset of the sample path space

\widetilde{\Omega}\subset\Omega
, such that

{\rm P}[\widetilde{\Omega}]=1
and
for any
\omega\in\widetilde{\Omega}
,

B(t,\omega)
is a real continuous function of
t
.
We say that
B(t)
has
a

continuous path
almost surely
(a.s., for short).

(BM3)
For an arbitrary
M\in\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,3,\dots\}
,
and for any sequence of times,

t_{0}:=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{M}
,
the increments
B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1}),m=1,2,\dots,M,

are independent,
and each increment is
in
the normal distribution

(
the Gaussian distribution
)
with mean 0 and variance
\sigma^{2}=t_{m}-t_{m-1}
.
It means that for any
1\leq m\leq M
and
a<b
,

{\rm P}[B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1})\in[a,b]]=\int_{a}^{b}p(t_{m}-t_{m-1},z|0)dz,

where we define for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}

p(t,y|x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}e^{-(x-y)^{2}/2t}},&\quad\mbox{for $t>0$},\cr\delta(x-y),&\quad\mbox{for $t=0$}.\end{array}\right.

(1.1)

Unless otherwise noted,
the one-dimensional standard Brownian motion is
simply abbreviated to BM in this lecture note.
The probability measure
{\rm P}
for the BM
in particular is called the

Wiener measure
.
The expectation with respect to the probability measure
{\rm P}

is denoted by
{\rm E}
.
We write the conditional probability as

{\rm P}[\cdot|C]
,
where
C
denotes the condition.
The conditional expectation is similarly
written as
{\rm E}[\cdot|C]
.

The third property
(BM3)
implies that
for any
0\leq s\leq t<\infty

{\rm P}[B(t)\in A|B(s)=x]=\int_{A}p(t-s,y|x)dy

(1.2)

holds,
{}^{\forall}A\in{\cal B}(\mathbb{R}),^{\forall}x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore the integral kernel
p(t,y|x)
given by (
1.1
)
is called the

transition probability density function

of Brownian motion starting from
x
.
The probability that the BM is observed
in a region
A_{m}\in{\cal B}(\mathbb{R})
at time
t_{m}

for each
m=1,2,\dots,M
is then given by

{\rm P}[B(t_{m})\in A_{m},m=1,2,\dots,M]=\int_{A_{1}}dx_{1}\cdots\int_{A_{M}}dx_{M}\,\prod_{m=1}^{M}p(t_{m}-t_{m-1},x_{m}|x_{m-1}),

(1.3)

where
x_{0}:=0
.

By
(BM3)
, we can see that, for any
c>0
,
the probability distribution of
B(c^{2}t)/c
is
equivalent with that of
B(t)
at arbitrary time
t\geq 0
.
It is written as

\frac{1}{c}B(c^{2}t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}B(t),\quad^{\forall}c>0,

where the symbol
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}
is for

equivalence in distribution
.
Moreover, (
1.3
) implies that,
for any
c>0
,
B(t),t\geq 0
and
its time changed process with
t\mapsto c^{2}t
multiplied by
a factor
1/c
(dilatation) follow the same probability law.
This

equivalence in probability law

of stochastic processes is expressed as

(B(t))_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(law)}}{{=}}\left(\frac{1}{c}B(c^{2}t)\right)_{t\geq 0},\quad^{\forall}c\geq 0,

(1.4)

and called the

scaling property of Brownian motion
.

For
a>0
, let

T_{a}=\inf\{t\geq 0:B(t)=a\}
. Then for any
t\geq 0
,

{\rm P}[T_{a}<t,B(t)<a]={\rm P}[T_{a}<t,B(t)>a],

(1.5)

since the transition probability density (
1.1
) is a symmetric function
of the increment
y-x
.
This property is called the

reflection principle
of BM.
For
\{\omega:B(t)>a\}\subset\{\omega:T_{a}<t\}
,
a>0
,
the above is equal to
{\rm P}[B(t)>a]
.

The formula (
1.3
) also means that for any fixed
s\geq 0
,
under the condition that
B(s)
is given,

\{B(t):t\leq s\}
and
\{B(t):t>s\}

are independent.
This independence of the events in the future
and those in the past is called the

Markov property
.
A positive random variable
\tau
is called

stopping time

(or
Markov time
),
if the event
\{\omega:\tau\leq t\}
is determined
by the behavior of the process
until time
t
and independent of that after
t
.
For any stopping time
\tau
,

\{B(t):t\leq\tau\}
and
\{B(t):t>\tau\}

are independent.
It is called the

strong Markov property
.
A stochastic process which has the strong Markov property
and has a continuous path almost surely
is generally called a

diffusion process
.

For each time
t\in[0,\infty)
,
we write the smallest
\sigma
-field generated by
the BM up to time
t\geq 0
as

\sigma(B(s):0\leq s\leq t)
and define

{\cal F}_{t}:=\sigma(B(s):0\leq s\leq t),\quad t\geq 0.

(1.6)

By definition, with respect to any event in
{\cal F}_{t}
,

B(s)
is measurable at every
s\in[0,t]
.
Then we have a nondecreasing family

\{{\cal F}_{t}:t\geq 0\}
of sub-
\sigma
-fields
of the original
\sigma
-field
{\cal F}
in the probability space

(\Omega,{\cal F},{\rm P})
such that

{\cal F}_{s}\subset{\cal F}_{t}\subset{\cal F}
for

0\leq s<t<\infty
.
We call this family of
\sigma
-fields
a
filtration
.

Since the probability density of increment
in any time-interval
t-s>0
,
p(t-s,z|0)
,
has mean zero,
BM satisfies the equality

{\rm E}[B(t)|{\cal F}_{s}]=B(s),\quad 0\leq s<t<\infty,\quad\mbox{a.s.}

(1.7)

That is, the mean is constant in time,
even though the variance
increases in time as
\sigma^{2}=t
.
Processes with such a property are called

martingales
.
We note that for
0\leq s<t<\infty

\displaystyle{\rm E}[B(t)^{2}|{\cal F}_{s}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm E}[(B(t)-B(s))^{2}+2(B(t)-B(s))B(s)+B(s)^{2}|{\cal F}_{s}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm E}[(B(t)-B(s))^{2}|{\cal F}_{s}]+2{\rm E}[(B(t)-B(s))B(s)|{\cal F}_{s}]+{\rm E}[B(s)^{2}|{\cal F}_{s}].

By the property
(BM3)
and the definition of
{\cal F}_{s}
,

\displaystyle{\rm E}[(B(t)-B(s))^{2}|{\cal F}_{s}]=t-s,

\displaystyle{\rm E}[(B(t)-B(s))B(s)|{\cal F}_{s}]={\rm E}[B(t)-B(s)|{\cal F}_{s}]\,B(s)=0,

\displaystyle{\rm E}[B(s)^{2}|{\cal F}_{s}]=B(s)^{2}.

Then we have the equality

{\rm E}[B(t)^{2}-t|{\cal F}_{s}]=B(s)^{2}-s,\quad 0\leq s<t<\infty,\quad\mbox{a.s.}

(1.8)

It means that
B(t)^{2}-t
is a martingale.

For the transition probability density of BM (
1.1
),
it should be noted that
p(\cdot,y|x)=p(\cdot,x|y)

for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
and
u_{t}(x):=p(t,y|x)

is a unique solution of the

heat equation

(
diffusion equation
)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}u_{t}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0

(1.9)

with the initial condition
u_{0}(x)=\delta(x-y)
.
The solution of (
1.9
) with the initial condition

u^{f}_{0}(x)=f(x),x\in\mathbb{R}
is then given by

u^{f}_{t}(x)={\rm E}^{x}[f(B(t))]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(y)p(t,y|x)dy,

(1.10)

if
f
is a measurable function
satisfying the condition

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-ax^{2}}|f(x)|dx<\infty

for some
a>0
.
Since
p(t,y|x)
plays as an integral kernel in (
1.10
),
it is also called the

heat kernel
.

For
0\leq s<t<\infty
,
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
, consider

{\rm E}[e^{\sqrt{-1}\xi(B(t)-B(s))}|{\cal F}_{s}]
.
It is calculated as
the
Fourier transform of the
transition probability density

p(t-s,\cdot|0)
;

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\sqrt{-1}\xi z}p(t-s,z|0)dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\sqrt{-1}\xi z}\frac{e^{-z^{2}/2(t-s)}}{\sqrt{2\pi(t-s)}}dz

(1.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{-\xi^{2}(t-s)/2}.

The obtained function of
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
,

{\rm E}[e^{\sqrt{-1}\xi(B(t)-B(s))}|{\cal F}_{s}]=e^{-\xi^{2}(t-s)/2},\quad 0\leq s<t<\infty,

(1.12)

is called the

characteristic function
of BM.

1.2
Itô’s formula and Kolmogorov equation

Let
X(t),t\geq 0
be a one-dimensional diffusion process
on the probability space
(\Omega_{X},{\cal F}_{X},{\rm P}_{X})
,
where the expectation is written as
{\rm E}_{X}

and the filtration is given by the

natural filtration
of
X
;

({\cal F}_{X})_{t}=\sigma(X(s):0\leq s\leq t),t\geq 0
.
For each time interval
[0,t],t>0
, put
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
let
\Delta_{n}=\Delta_{n}([0,t])
be a subdivision of
[0,t]
with

t_{0}:=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n-1}<t_{n}:=t
.
Then we define

Q^{\Delta_{n}}(t)=\sum_{m=1}^{n}(X(t_{m})-X(t_{m-1}))^{2}
.
If there is a process
Q(t),t\geq 0
such that

\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm P}_{X}[|Q^{\Delta_{n}}(t)-Q(t)|>\varepsilon]=0,\quad^{\forall}\varepsilon>0

(1.13)

holds provided

\max_{1\leq m\leq n}|t_{m}-t_{m-1}|\to 0

as
n\to\infty
,
then we call
Q(t)
,
t\geq 0
,
the

quadratic variation
of
X(t),t\geq 0

and express it by

\langle X,X\rangle_{t},t\geq 0
.

For BM,
B(t),t\geq 0
, set
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

t_{0}:=0<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{n-1}<t_{n}:=t

and put

Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)=\sum_{m=1}^{n}(B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1}))^{2}.

By the property
(BM3)
, the mean is given by

{\rm E}[Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)]=\sum_{m=1}^{n}(t_{m}-t_{m-1})=t.

The variance of
Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)

\displaystyle\sigma^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle{\rm E}[(Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)-t)^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm E}\left[\left\{\sum_{m=1}^{n}\Big\{(B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1}))^{2}-(t_{m}-t_{m-1})\Big\}\right\}^{2}\right]

is calculated as

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n}\Big\{{\rm E}[(B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1}))^{4}]-2(t_{m}-t_{m-1}){\rm E}[(B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1}))^{2}]

(1.14)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(t_{m}-t_{m-1})^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{n}\Big\{3(t_{m}-t_{m-1})^{2}-2(t_{m}-t_{m-1})^{2}+(t_{m}-t_{m-1})^{2}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\sum_{m=1}^{n}(t_{m}-t_{m-1})^{2}\leq 2t\max_{1\leq m\leq n}|t_{m}-t_{m-1}|,

where
independence of increments of BM mentioned in
(BM3)
and

{\rm E}[(B(t)-B(s))^{4}]=3(t-s)^{2},\quad 0\leq s\leq t

(1.15)

were used.
By

Chebyshev’s inequality
, we have

{\rm P}[|Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)-t|>\varepsilon]\leq\frac{\sigma^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}},\quad^{\forall}\varepsilon>0.

Provided that
\max_{1\leq m\leq n}|t_{m}-t_{m-1}|\to 0

as
n\to\infty
,
(
1.14
) gives
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sigma^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)=0

and it proves

\langle B,B\rangle_{t}=t,\quad\quad t\geq 0.

(1.16)

For a stopping time
\tau
, we put

X^{\tau}(t):=X(t\wedge\tau),t\geq 0
,
where
t\wedge\tau:=\min\{t,\tau\}
.
We define a diffusion process
X(t),t\geq 0

as a
local martingale
,
if there exists stopping times
\tau_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
(i) the sequence
\{\tau_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is nondecreasing
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}=\infty
a.s., and
(ii) for every
n
, the process

X^{\tau_{n}}(t),t\geq 0

is a martingale.
When
X(t)
is a local martingale,
we can prove that a unique increasing continuous process
is given by
\langle X,X\rangle_{t},t\geq 0

such that
\langle X,X\rangle_{0}=0
and

X(t)^{2}-\langle X,X\rangle_{t},t\geq 0

provides a local martingale.

Assume that
X(t)
and
Y(t),t\geq 0

are both local martingales.
Then
(X(t)+Y(t))^{2}-\langle X+Y,X+Y\rangle_{t}

and
(X(t)-Y(t))^{2}-\langle X-Y,X-Y\rangle_{t}
,
t\geq 0

are local martingales.
Therefore, their difference

4X(t)Y(t)-\{\langle X+Y,X+Y\rangle_{t}-\langle X-Y,X-Y\rangle_{t}\},t\geq 0

is also a local martingale.
For any pair of local martingales
X(t)
and
Y(t),t\geq 0
, we define
the

mutual quadratic variation
(
cross variation
)
process
as

\langle X,Y\rangle_{t}:=\frac{1}{4}\{\langle X+Y,X+Y\rangle_{t}-\langle X-Y,X-Y\rangle_{t}\},\quad t\geq 0.

(1.17)

We can prove that,
if
B_{i}(t),t\geq 0,i=1,2,\dots,D

are independent BMs, then

\langle B_{i},B_{j}\rangle_{t}=\delta_{ij}t,\quad i,j=1,\dots,D,\quad t\geq 0.

(1.18)

For a continuous process
A(t),t\geq 0
, here
we consider the quantity

S^{\Delta_{n}}(t)=\sum_{m=1}^{n}|A(t_{m})-A(t_{m-1})|

instead of
Q^{\Delta_{n}}(t)
,
where
\Delta_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
is a subdivision
of the time interval
[0,t]
.
We can see that if
\Delta_{n}\subset\Delta_{n+1}
,
then
S^{\Delta_{n}}(t)\leq S^{\Delta_{n+1}}(t),^{\forall}t\geq 0
.
Assume that
\max_{1\leq m\leq n}|t_{m}-t_{m-1}|\to 0

as
n\to\infty
.
Let
\lim_{n\to\infty}\sup_{\Delta_{n}}S^{\Delta_{n}}(t)=S(t)\leq\infty

and call it the

variation
of
A
on
[0,t]
.
If
S(t)<\infty
for every
t
, then
the process
A(t),t\geq 0
is of

finite variation
.
Let
S_{\rm BM}(t),t\geq 0
be the variation of BM.
We have

Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)\leq\sup_{1\leq m\leq n}|B(t_{m})-B(t_{m-1})|S_{\rm BM}(t),\quad t\geq 0,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}.

By the property
(BM2)
,
RHS becomes 0 as
n\to\infty
if
S_{\rm BM}(t)
is
finite.
On the other hand, we have proved

Q^{\Delta_{n}}_{\rm BM}(t)\to\langle B,B\rangle_{t}

as
n\to\infty
in probability and the fact (
1.16
).
Hence
S_{\rm BM}(t)=\infty
a.s.
{}^{\forall}t\geq 0
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\{X_{1}(t),\dots,X_{N}(t)\}
,
t\geq 0

be a set of diffusion processes.
Put
\bm{X}(t)=(X_{1}(t),\dots,X_{N}(t))
.
Let
F
be a real function
of
(t,\bm{x})\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
which is bounded and has a bounded first-order derivative with respect to
t
and
bounded first- and
second-order derivatives with respect to
x_{j},1\leq j\leq N
,
and we denote this by
F\in{\rm C}^{1,2}_{\rm b}
.
We know that,
in order to describe the statistics of a function
of several random variables, we have to take into account
the ‘propagation of error’.
For the process
F(t,\bm{X}(t))

that is defined as a function of
t
as well as a functional of
processes
X_{1}(t),\dots,X_{N}(t)
,
t\geq 0
,

Itô’s formula
gives an equation which
governs the difference of
F(t,\bm{X}(\cdot))
as

\displaystyle dF(t,\bm{X}(t))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial F}{\partial x_{i}}(t,\bm{X}(t))dX_{i}(t)+\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}(t,\bm{X}(t))dt

(1.19)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}(t,\bm{X}(t))d\langle X_{i},X_{j}\rangle_{t},\quad t\geq 0.

The first term gives a local martingale
and the second and third terms give processes of finite variations.
A continuous process
X

given by the sum of a local martingale
M

and a finite-variation process
A
,

X(t)=M(t)+A(t),\quad t\geq 0,

is called a

semi-martingale
.
We will use the fact that if the continuous process

F(t,\bm{X}(t)),\ t\geq 0
is a local martingale, then
its finite-variation part should vanish,
and vice versa.

Let
X(t),t\geq 0
be a one-dimensional diffusion
in
(\Omega_{X},{\cal F}_{X},{\rm P}_{X})
,
which satisfies the following SDE

dX(t)=\sigma(X(t))dB(t)+b(X(t))dt,\quad X(0)=x.

(1.20)

Here
B(t),t\geq 0
is BM and the functions

\sigma,b:\mathbb{R}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfy
the condition that

{}^{\exists}K\geq 0
, s.t.

|\sigma(x)-\sigma(y)|\leq K|x-y|
,

|b(x)-b(y)|\leq K|x-y|,x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.
(This is called the

Lipschitz continuity
.)
Put

u(s,x)={\rm E}^{x}[f(X(T-s))],\quad 0\leq s<T<\infty,

(1.21)

with an
({\cal F}_{X})_{T}
-measurable bounded function
f
.
By the Markov property,
for
0\leq s<t<T<\infty
,

\displaystyle u(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm E}^{x}\Big[{\rm E}^{X(t-s)}[f(X(T-t))]\Big]

(1.22)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm E}^{x}[u(t,X(t-s))].

Assume that
u(s,x)\in{\rm C}^{1,2}_{\rm b}
.
Then by Itô’s formula (
1.19
),

\displaystyle u(t,X(t-s))-u(s,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t-s}\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+{\sf L}u\right)(s+r,X(r))dr

(1.23)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t-s}\sigma(X(r))\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}(s+r,X(r))dB(r),

where

({\sf L}f)(x)=\frac{1}{2}a(x)\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}f(x)+b(x)\frac{d}{dx}f(x)

(1.24)

with

a(x)=\sigma(x)^{2}.

(1.25)

Since (
1.22
) holds, taking expectation of (
1.23
) gives

{\rm E}^{x}\left[\int_{0}^{t-s}\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+{\sf L}u\right)(s+r,X(r))dr\right]=0,

where the expectation of the second term
in RHS of (
1.23
) vanished
for it is a martingale.
Set
h=t-s
, divide the both side of the above equation
by
h
and take the limit
h\to 0
.
Then we have

\frac{\partial u(s,x)}{\partial s}+{\sf L}u(s,x)=0.

(1.26)

Since
{\sf L}
acts as a differential operator
with respect to the initial value
x
,
it is called the

backward Kolmogorov equation
.
The differential operator (
1.24
)
is called the

generator
of the diffusion process.

1.3
Martingale polynomials of BM

Let

G_{\alpha}(t,x)=e^{\alpha x-\alpha^{2}t/2}.

(1.27)

For BM, we perform the transformation
with the parameter

\alpha\in\mathbb{C}:=\{z=x+\sqrt{-1}y:x,y\in\mathbb{R}\}
,

B\mapsto\check{B}_{\alpha}
defined by

\check{B}_{\alpha}(t)=G_{\alpha}(t,B(t)),\quad t\geq 0,

(1.28)

which is called the

Esscher transformation
.
Since

{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(t)}]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\alpha x}p(t,x|0)dx=e^{\alpha^{2}t/2},\quad t\geq 0.

we can see that

G_{\alpha}(t,B(t))=\frac{e^{\alpha B(t)}}{{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(t)}]},\quad t\geq 0,

(1.29)

and hence

\displaystyle{\rm E}[G_{\alpha}(t,B(t))|{\cal F}_{s}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(t)}|{\cal F}_{s}]}{{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(t)}]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(s)}e^{\alpha(B(t)-B(s))}|{\cal F}_{s}]}{{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(s)}e^{\alpha(B(t)-B(s))}]},\quad 0<s<t.

By the definition of
{\cal F}_{s}
and independence of increment
of BM (the property
(BM3)
),
the numerator is equal to
e^{\alpha B(s)}{\rm E}[e^{\alpha(B(t)-B(s))}]
,
and the denominator is equal to
{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(s)}]{\rm E}[e^{\alpha(B(t)-B(s))}]
.
Hence the above equals

e^{\alpha B(s)}/{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(s)}]=G_{\alpha}(s,B(s))
.
Therefore,
G_{\alpha}(t,B(t))
is a martingale:

{\rm E}[G_{\alpha}(t,B(t))|{\cal F}_{s}]=G_{\alpha}(s,B(s)),\quad 0\leq s\leq t.

(1.30)

The function (
1.27
) is expanded as

G_{\alpha}(t,x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}m_{n}(t,x)\frac{\alpha^{n}}{n!}

(1.31)

with

m_{n}(t,x)=\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^{n/2}H_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2t}}\right),\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\{0,1,2,\dots\}.

(1.32)

Here
\{H_{n}(x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are the

Hermite polynomials
of degrees

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

\displaystyle H_{n}(x):=(-1)^{n}e^{x^{2}}\frac{d^{n}e^{-x^{2}}}{dx^{n}}=\sum_{k=0}^{[n/2]}(-1)^{k}\frac{n!}{k!(n-2k)!}(2x)^{n-2k},

(1.33)

where for
a\geq 0
,
[a]
denotes the largest integer
which is not larger than
a
.
We can show the following contour integral representations
of the Hermite polynomials,

H_{n}(z)=\frac{n!}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{C(\delta_{0})}d\eta\,\frac{e^{2\eta z-\eta^{2}}}{\eta^{n+1}},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(1.34)

where
C(\delta_{0})
is a closed contour on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}

encircling the origin 0 once in the positive direction.

Lemma 1.1

The functions
\{m_{n}(t,x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
satisfy the following.

(i) They are monic polynomials of degrees
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

with time-dependent coefficients:

m_{n}(t,x)=x^{n}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}c_{n}^{(k)}(t)x^{k},\quad t\geq 0.

(ii) For
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
c_{n}^{(k)}(0)=0
. That is,

m_{n}(0,x)=x^{n},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(iii) If we set
x=B(t)
, they provide martingales:

{\rm E}[m_{n}(t,B(t))|{\cal F}_{s}]=m_{n}(s,B(s)),\quad 0\leq s\leq t,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(1.35)

We call
\{m_{n}(t,x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

the
fundamental martingale polynomials

associated with BM
[
30
]
.
For
n=2
, (
1.33
) gives

H_{2}(x)=4x^{2}-2
, and then

m_{2}(t,x)=x^{2}-t
by (
1.32
).
We already proved in (
1.8
)
that
m_{2}(t,B(t))=B(t)^{2}-t
is a martingale.

The Fourier transformation of
G_{\alpha}(t,x)
with respect to
the parameter
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
is calculated as

\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\sqrt{-1}\alpha w}}{2\pi}G_{\alpha}(t,x)d\alpha=\frac{e^{-(\sqrt{-1}x+w)^{2}/2t}}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}.

(1.36)

Owing to the factor
e^{-w^{2}/2t}
in
\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)
,
the following calculations are justified,

\displaystyle G_{\alpha}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\sqrt{-1}\alpha w}\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)dw=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{n}}{n!}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sqrt{-1}w)^{n}\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)dw,

(1.37)

which proves the integral representation of

m_{n}(t,x)
,

\displaystyle m_{n}(t,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sqrt{-1}w)^{n}\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)dw

(1.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sqrt{-1}w)^{n}\frac{e^{-(\sqrt{-1}x+w)^{2}/2t}}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}dw,\quad t\geq 0,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

We define this type of integral transformation of a function
f
as

{\cal I}[f(W)|(t,x)]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(\sqrt{-1}w)\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)dw.

(1.39)

Then the above results are written as

m_{n}(t,x)={\cal I}[W^{n}|(t,x)],\quad t\geq 0,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(1.40)

1.4
Bessel processes (BES
D
)

1.4.1
Radial part of
D
-dimensional BM

Let
D\in\mathbb{N}
denote the spatial dimension.
For
D\geq 2
, the
D
-dimensional BM in
\mathbb{R}^{D}
starting
from the position
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{D})\in\mathbb{R}^{D}

is defined by the
D
-dimensional
vector-valued diffusion process,

\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)=(B^{x_{1}}_{1}(t),B^{x_{2}}_{2}(t),\dots,B^{x_{D}}_{D}(t)),\quad t\geq 0,

(1.41)

where

B^{x_{i}}_{i}(t):=x_{i}+B_{i}(t),\quad t\geq 0,\quad i=1,\dots,D

are independent one-dimensional BMs
starting from
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.

The
D
-dimensional

Bessel process

is defined as the absolute value
(
i.e.
, the radial coordinate) of the

D
-dimensional Brownian motion,

\displaystyle R^{x}(t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle|\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)|

(1.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{B^{x_{1}}_{1}(t)^{2}+\cdots+B^{x_{D}}_{D}(t)^{2}},\quad t\geq 0,

where the initial value is given by

R^{x}(0)=x=|\bm{x}|=\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+\dots+x_{D}^{2}}\geq 0
.
By definition
R^{x}(t)
is nonnegative,

R^{x}(t)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{0\}
,
where
\mathbb{R}_{+}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x>0\}
.
We will abbreviate the
D
-dimensional Bessel process
to BES
D
.

By this definition,
R^{x}(t)
is a functional of

D
-tuples of diffusion processes

\{B^{x_{i}}_{i}(t)\}_{i=1}^{D}
,
t\geq 0
.
Now we apply Itô’s formula (
1.19
)
to BES
D
(
1.42
).
Assume
x=|\bm{x}|>0
.
In this case

R^{x}(t)=F(\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)),\quad t\geq 0\quad\mbox{with}\quad F(\bm{y})=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{D}y_{i}^{2}}.

(1.43)

We see that

\frac{\partial F}{\partial t}=0,\quad\frac{\partial F}{\partial y_{i}}=\frac{y_{i}}{F},\quad\frac{\partial^{2}F}{\partial y_{i}\partial y_{j}}=\frac{\delta_{ij}}{F}-\frac{y_{i}y_{j}}{F^{3}},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq D.

From (
1.18
), we have

d\langle B_{i}^{x_{i}},B_{j}^{x_{j}}\rangle_{t}=\langle dB_{i}^{x_{i}},dB_{j}^{x_{j}}\rangle_{t}=\delta_{ij}dt
,
1\leq i,j\leq D,t\geq 0
.
Then, the second term of (
1.19
) for BES
D

becomes

\frac{1}{2}\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq D}\left\{\frac{\delta_{ij}}{F(\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t))}-\frac{B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t)B_{j}^{x_{j}}(t)}{F(\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t))^{3}}\right\}\delta_{ij}dt=\frac{D-1}{2}\frac{1}{F(\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t))}dt=\frac{D-1}{2}\frac{dt}{R^{x}(t)}.

On the other hand, the first term of (
1.19
) for BES
D
is

\frac{1}{R^{x}(t)}\sum_{i=1}^{D}B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t)dB_{i}^{x_{i}}(t).

(1.44)

It seems to be complicated, but the quadratic variation is calculated as

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{R^{x}}\sum_{i=1}^{D}B_{i}^{x_{i}}dB_{i}^{x_{i}},\frac{1}{R^{x}}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{j}^{x_{j}}dB_{j}^{x_{j}}\right\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R^{x}(t)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t)B_{j}^{x_{j}}(t)\langle dB_{i}^{x_{i}},dB_{j}^{x_{j}}\rangle_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R^{x}(t)^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{D}\sum_{j=1}^{D}B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t)B_{j}^{x_{j}}(t)\delta_{ij}dt=dt,

where the independence of BMs (
1.18
)
and the definition,
R^{x}(t)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{D}B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t)^{2}
,
have been used.
That is, (
1.44
) is equivalent in probability law with
an infinitesimal increment of a diffusion process with
quadratic variation
dt
.
Then, by introducing a BM,
B^{x}(t),t\geq 0
,
which is different from
B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t),t\geq 0,x_{i}\in\mathbb{R},i=1,2,\dots,D

and is started at
x=|\bm{x}|>0
,
(
1.44
) is identified with
dB^{x}(t),t\geq 0
.
Hence we have obtained the following equation for BES
D
,

dR^{x}(t)=dB^{x}(t)+\frac{D-1}{2}\frac{dt}{R^{x}(t)},\quad x>0,\quad 0\leq t<T^{x},

(1.45)

where
T^{x}=\inf\{t>0:R^{x}(t)=0\}
.

The first term of RHS,
dB^{x}(t)
, denotes the infinitesimal
increment of BM starting
from
x>0
at time
t=0
.
This martingale term gives randomness to the motion.
On the other hand, if
D>1
,
for
dt>0
, the second term in RHS of (
1.45
)
is positive definite.
It means that there is a drift to increase the value of
R^{x}(t)
.
This drift term is increasing in
D
and decreasing in
R^{x}(t)
.
Since as
R^{x}(t)\downarrow 0
, the drift term

\uparrow\infty
, it seems that
a ‘repulsive force’ is acting to the
D
-dimensional BM,

\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t),|\bm{x}|>0
to keep the distance
from the origin be positive,
R^{x}(t)=|\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)|>0

and avoid a collision of the Brownian particle
with the origin.
A differential equation such as (
1.45
), which involves
a random fluctuation term and a drift term is called a

stochastic differential equation
(SDE).

The following equivalence in probability law is established
for arbitrary
x>0
,

\left(\frac{1}{x}R^{x}(x^{2}t)\right)_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(law)}}{{=}}(R^{1}(t))_{t\geq 0}.

(1.46)

It inherits (
1.4
) and is called
the
scaling property of the Bessel process
.

The SDE for BES
D
is given by the equation (
1.20
)
with
\sigma(x):=1
and
b(x)=(D-1)/(2x)
.
Then the generator of BES
D
is obtained as

{\sf L}^{D}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{D-1}{2x}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}.

(1.47)

Let
p_{D}(t-s,y|x)
be the transition probability density of
BES
D
from
x
at times
s\geq 0

to
y
at times
t\geq s
.
For any
t\geq s,y\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

p_{D}(t-s,y|x),x>0
solves
(
1.26
) with (
1.47
)
under the condition

\lim_{s\uparrow t}p_{D}(t-s,y|x)=\delta(x-y)
.
In other words,

p_{D}(t,y|x)
solves

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{D}(t,y|x)={\sf L}_{D}p_{D}(t,y|x)

(1.48)

under the initial condition
p_{D}(0,y|x)=\delta(x-y)
,
which is called the backward Kolmogorov equation
for BES
D
.

Let
I_{\nu}(z)
be the

modified Bessel function

of the first kind defined by

I_{\nu}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\Gamma(n+1)\Gamma(n+1+\nu)}\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{2n+\nu}

(1.49)

with the gamma function

\Gamma(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-u}u^{z-1}du,\quad{\rm Re}\,z>0.

(1.50)

The function
I_{\nu}(z)
solves the

Bessel differential equation

\frac{d^{2}w}{dz^{2}}+\frac{1}{z}\frac{dw}{dz}-\left(1+\frac{\nu^{2}}{z^{2}}\right)w=0.

(1.51)

Then we can show that

p_{D}(t,y|x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\frac{1}{t}\frac{y^{\nu+1}}{x^{\nu}}e^{-(x^{2}+y^{2})/2t}I_{\nu}\left(\frac{xy}{t}\right)},&\quad t>0,\,x>0,\,y\geq 0,\cr\displaystyle{\frac{y^{2\nu+1}}{2^{\nu}t^{\nu+1}\Gamma(\nu+1)}e^{-y^{2}/2t}},&\quad t>0,\,x=0,\,y\geq 0\cr&\cr\delta(y-x),&\quad t=0,\,x,y\geq 0,\end{array}\right.

(1.52)

where the index
\nu
is specified by the dimension
D
as

\nu=\frac{D-2}{2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad D=2(\nu+1).

(1.53)

This fact that
p_{D}(t,y|x)
is expressed by
using
I_{\nu}(z)
gives the reason why
the process
R^{x}(t)
is called the Bessel process.

1.4.2
BES
3
and absorbing BM

When
D=3
,
\nu=1/2
by (
1.53
),
and we can use the equality

I_{1/2}(z)=\sqrt{2/\pi z}\sinh z

=(e^{z}-e^{-z})/\sqrt{2\pi z}
.
Then (
1.52
) gives

p_{3}(t,y|x)=\frac{y}{x}\Big\{p(t,y|x)-p(t,y|-x)\Big\}

(1.54)

for
t>0,x>0,y\geq 0
, where
p(t,y|x)
is
the transition probability density of BM started at
x
given by (
1.1
).
If we put

q_{\rm abs}(t,y|x)=p(t,y|x)-p(t,y|-x),

(1.55)

we see that

q_{\rm abs}(t,0|x)=0
for any
x>0
,
since the transition probability density of BM,
p(t,y|x)
,
is an even function of
y-x
.

Figure 1:

One realization of a Brownian path
from
x>0
to
y>0
is represented by a red curve and denoted by path A,
which visits the nonpositive region
\mathbb{R}_{-}\cup\{0\}
, where

\mathbb{R}_{-}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:x<0\}
.
The first time the path A hits the origin is
denoted by
\tau
.
Path B, which is represented by a black curve, is a mirror image of path A with respect
to the origin
x=0
, which is running from
-x<0
to
-y<0
.
The path C (blue curve) is then defined as
the concatenation of the part of path B from time zero
up to time
\tau

and the part of path A after
\tau

such that it runs from
-x<0
to
y>0
.
By the reflection principle of BM (
1.5
)
applied at time
\tau
, we see that
there exists a bijection between path A and path C,
which have the same probability weight as Brownian paths.
Since the Brownian path A contributes
to
p(t,y|x)
and the Brownian path C contributes to
p(t,y|-x)
,
such a path from
x>0
to
y>0
that visits
\mathbb{R}_{-}
is cancelled
in
q_{\rm abs}(t,y|x)
given by (
1.55
).
In Fig.
1
(b), a path from
x>0
to
y>0

which stays in the positive region
\mathbb{R}_{+}

is considered (path A).
In this case there is no such a path
that perfectly cancels the contribution of path A to (
1.55
)
as path B in the case (a).
In summary,
q_{\rm abs}(t,y|x)=p(t,y|x)-p(t,y|-x)

gives the total weight of Brownian paths
which do not hit the origin.

We consider the situation where an absorbing wall is put at the origin and,
if the Brownian particle starting from
x>0
arrives at the origin,
it is absorbed there and the motion is stopped.
Such a process is called
the

absorbing Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
Its transition probability density is given by

q_{\rm abs}
.

By absorption, the total mass of paths from
x>0
to
y>0

is then reduced, if we compare the
original BM and the absorbing Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
The factor
y/x
appearing in the transition probability density
(
1.54
) of BES
3
is for
renormalization so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}p_{3}(t,y|x)dy=1,\quad^{\forall}t>0,\quad^{\forall}x>0.

(1.56)

We regard this renormalization procedure from
q_{\rm abs}

to
p_{3}
as a transformation.
Since

h_{1}(x)=x

(1.57)

is a one-dimensional harmonic function
in a rather trivial sense

\Delta^{(1)}h_{1}(x):=\frac{d^{2}x}{dx^{2}}=0,

we say that the BES
3
is
a
harmonic transformation

(
h
-transformation) of the one-dimensional absorbing BM
in the sense of Doob.
This implies the following equivalence.

BES
3

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

one-dimensional Brownian motion
conditioned to stay positive

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

h
-transform of absorbing BM in
\mathbb{R}_{+}

Let
\mathbb{E}_{{\rm BES}_{3}}^{x}
denote the expectation
with respect to BES
3
,
(R(t))_{t\geq 0}
, started at
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
For an independent BM,
(B(t))_{t\geq 0}
started at the same point
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
let
\tau=\inf\{t>0:B(t)=0\}
.
Then the above equivalence is written as follows;
for any
{\cal F}_{t}
-measurable bounded function

F
,
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\rm BES}_{3}}^{x}[F(R(t))]

\displaystyle={\rm E}^{x}\left[F(B(t)){\bf 1}_{(\tau>t)}\frac{B(t)}{x}\right]

\displaystyle={\rm E}^{x}\left[F(B(t)){\bf 1}_{(\tau>t)}\frac{h_{1}(B(t)}{h_{1}(x)}\right],\quad t\geq 0,

(1.58)

where
{\rm E}^{x}
is an expectation with respect to
BM started at
x\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
If
F
is an even function;
F(-x)=F(x)
, then
the above gives

\mathbb{E}_{{\rm BES}_{3}}^{x}[F(R(t))]={\rm E}^{x}\left[F(B(t))\frac{h_{1}(B(t))}{h_{1}(x)}\right],\quad t\geq 0,

(1.59)

since by the reflection principle of BM
(
1.5
),
all contribution from paths
\{\omega:\tau\leq t\}

should be canceled out.

Here we emphasize the obvious fact that
p_{3}(t,0|x)=0,^{\forall}x>0
.
It implies that BES
3
does not
visit the origin.
When
D=3
, the outward drift is strong enough to avoid
any visit to the origin.
Moreover, we can prove that for any
x>0
,
R^{x}(t)\to\infty

as
t\to\infty
with probability 1
and we say the process is

transient

(see Theorem
1.2
(ii) below).

1.4.3
Critical dimension
D_{\rm c}=2

Originally, the Bessel process was defined by (
1.42
)
for
D\in\mathbb{N}
.
We find that, however, the modified Bessel function
(
1.49
) is an analytic function of

\nu
for all values of
\nu
.
So we will be able to define the Bessel process
for any value of dimension
D\geq 1

as a diffusion process in
\mathbb{R}_{+}

such that the transition probability density function
is given by (
1.52
), where the index

\nu\geq-1/2
is determined by (
1.53
)
for each value of
D\geq 1
.

For BES
D
starting from
x>0
, denote
its first visiting time at the origin by

T^{x}=\inf\{t>0:R^{x}(t)=0\}.

(1.60)

The following theorem is proved
(see, for instance,
[
31
]
).

Theorem 1.2

(i)
D\geq 2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T^{x}=\infty,^{\forall}x>0
, with probability 1.

(ii)
D>2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\displaystyle{\lim_{t\to\infty}}R^{x}(t)=\infty,\,^{\forall}x>0
, with probability 1,

i.e.
the process is transient.

(iii)
D=2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\displaystyle{\inf_{t>0}}\,R^{x}(t)=0,\,^{\forall}x>0
, with probability 1D

That is, BES
(2)
starting from
x>0
does not
visit the origin, but it can visit any neighborhood of
the origin.

(iv)
1\leq D<2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T^{x}<\infty,\,^{\forall}x>0
, with probability 1,

i.e.
the process is recurrent.

1.4.4
Bessel flow and
another critical dimension
\overline{D}_{\rm c}=3/2

Theorem
1.2
states that we have a

critical dimension
,

D_{\rm c}=2,

for competition between the two effects acting
the Bessel process,
the ‘random force’ (the martingale term)
and the ‘entropy force’ (the outward drift term)
in (
1.45
):
when
D>D_{\rm c}
, the latter dominates the former
and the process becomes transient, and when

D<D_{\rm c}
, the former is relevant and recurrence
to the origin of the process is realized frequently.

Here we show that there is another critical dimension
[
47
]
,

\overline{D}_{\rm c}=\frac{3}{2}.

In order to characterize the transition at
\overline{D}_{\rm c}
,
we have to investigate the dependence of the behavior
of
R^{x}(t)
on its initial value,
x>0
.
We call the one-parameter family

\{R^{x}(t):t\geq 0\}_{x>0}
the

Bessel flow

for each fixed
D>1
.

For
0<x<y
, we trace the motions of two BES
D
’s
starting from
x
and
y
by solving
(
1.45
) using the
common
BM,
B(t),t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle R^{x}(t)=x+B(t)+\frac{D-1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{R^{x}(s)},

\displaystyle R^{y}(t)=y+B(t)+\frac{D-1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{ds}{R^{y}(s)},\quad 0\leq t<T^{x}.

(1.61)

We will see that

\displaystyle x<y\quad

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad R^{x}(t)<R^{y}(t),\quad 0\leq t<T^{x}\quad\mbox{with probability 1}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow

\displaystyle\quad T^{x}\leq T^{y}\quad\mbox{with probability 1}.

The interesting fact is that in the
intermediate fractional dimensions,

\overline{D}_{\rm c}<D<D_{\rm c}
,
it is possible to have a situation where

T^{x}=T^{y}
even for
x<y
.

Figure 2:
In the intermediate fractional dimensions,

3/2<D<2
, there is a positive probability
that two Bessel processes starting from different
initial positions,
0<x<y<\infty
, return
to the origin simultaneously,
T^{x}=T^{y}
.

Theorem 1.3

For
0<x<y<\infty
,

(i)
1\leq D\leq 3/2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad T^{x}<T^{y}\quad\mbox{with probability 1}
.

(ii)
3/2<D<2\quad\Longrightarrow\quad{\rm P}[T^{x}=T^{y}]>0
.

1.5
Hermitian-matrix-valued BM and
the Dyson model with parameter
\beta
(DYS
β
)

1.5.1
Multivariate extensions of Bessel processes

Here we consider the stochastic motion of two particles

(X_{1}(t),X_{2}(t))
in one dimension
\mathbb{R}

satisfying the following SDEs,

\displaystyle dX_{1}(t)=dB_{1}(t)+\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{dt}{X_{1}(t)-X_{2}(t)},

\displaystyle dX_{2}(t)=dB_{2}(t)+\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{dt}{X_{2}(t)-X_{1}(t)},

(1.62)

with the initial condition
x_{1}=X_{1}(0)<x_{2}=X_{2}(0)

for
0\leq t<\inf\{t>0:X_{1}(t)=X_{2}(t)\}
,
where
B_{1}(t)
and
B_{2}(t)
,
t\geq 0
are independent BMs
and
\beta>0
is the ‘coupling constant’ of the two particles.
The second terms in (
1.62
)
represent the repulsive force acting between two
particles, which is proportional to the inverse
of the distance between them,
X_{2}(t)-X_{1}(t)
.
Since it is a central force (
i.e.
,
depending only on distance, and thus symmetric
for two particles), the ‘center of mass’

X_{\rm c}(t):=(X_{2}(t)+X_{1}(t))/2

is just a time change of BM;
we can calculate the quadratic variation as

d\langle X_{\rm c},X_{\rm c}\rangle_{t}=\langle dX_{\rm c},dX_{\rm c}\rangle_{t}=\langle(dB_{1}+dB_{2})/2,(dB_{1}+dB_{2})/2\rangle_{t}=dt/2
,
since
d\langle B_{1},B_{1}\rangle_{t}=\langle dB_{1},dB_{1}\rangle_{t}=dt
,

d\langle B_{2},B_{2}\rangle_{t}=\langle dB_{2},dB_{2}\rangle_{t}=dt
and

d\langle B_{1},B_{2}\rangle_{t}=\langle dB_{1},dB_{2}\rangle_{t}=0
.
Then

(X_{\rm c}(t))_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(law)}}{{=}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}B(t)\right)_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(law)}}{{=}}(B(t/2))_{t\geq 0},

where
B(t)
is a BM independent from
B_{1}(t)
and
B_{2}(t)
.
On the other hand, if we define the relative
coordinate by

X_{\rm r}(t):=(X_{2}(t)-X_{1}(t))/\sqrt{2}
,
it satisfies the SDE

dX_{\rm r}(t)=d\widetilde{B}(t)+\frac{\beta}{2}\frac{dt}{X_{\rm r}(t)}

(1.63)

for
0<t<\inf\{t>0:X_{\rm r}(t)=0\}
,
where
\widetilde{B}(t),\ t\geq 0
is a BM independent from

B_{1}(t),B_{2}(t),B(t)
,
t\geq 0
.
It is nothing but the SDE for BES
D
with

D=\beta+1
:

(X_{\rm r}(t))_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(law)}}{{=}}(R(t))_{t\geq 0},\quad D=\beta+1.

(1.64)

Now we consider the following
N
-particle systems
of
interacting Brownian motions

in
\mathbb{R}
as a solution

\bm{X}(t)=(X_{1}(t),X_{2}(t),\dots,X_{N}(t))

of the following system of SDEs:
with
\beta>0
and the condition

x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{N}

for initial positions
x_{i}=X_{i}(0),i=1,\dots,N
,

dX_{i}(t)=dB_{i}(t)+\frac{\beta}{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq N,\cr j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{dt}{X_{i}(t)-X_{j}(t)},\quad t\in[0,T^{\bm{x}}),\quad i=1,\dots,N,

(1.65)

where
\{B_{i}(t)\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
t\geq 0
are independent BMs
and

\displaystyle T^{\bm{x}}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\inf\{t>0:X_{i}(t)=X_{j}(t)\},\quad 1\leq i<j\leq N,

\displaystyle T^{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\min_{1\leq i<j\leq N}T^{\bm{x}}_{ij}.

It is usually called

Dyson’s Brownian motion model with parameter

\beta
.
But in this lecture we simply call it the

Dyson model with parameter

\beta

and denoted by DYS
β
.

As shown above, in the case where
N=2
, DYS
β

is a composition of a BM (the center of mass)
and a BES
(β+1)
(the relative coordinate).
In this sense, DYS
β
can be regarded
as a multivariate (multidimensional) extension
of BES
{}_{(\beta+1)},\ \beta>0
.

We can prove that,
for any
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
with
x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{N}
,

T^{\bm{x}}<\infty
if
\beta<1
,
and
T^{\bm{x}}=\infty
if
\beta\geq 1

[
65
,
23
]
.
The critical value
\beta_{\rm c}=1
seems to
correspond to
D_{\rm c}=\beta_{\rm c}+1=2
of
BES
D
.
Together with (
1.64
), this observation suggests the
following relation between parameters,

D=\beta+1\quad\iff\quad\beta=D-1.

(1.66)

In particular, we study the special case of
Dyson’s BM model with parameter
\beta=2
, DYS
2

As shown above, the case where
\beta=2

corresponds to a BES
D
with
D=3
.
In Section
1.4
, we have shown that
BES
3
has two aspects:

[Aspect 1]
as a radial coordinate of three-dimensional
Brownian motion, which was used to define the Bessel process
in Section
1.4.1
,
and

[Aspect 2]
as a one-dimensional Brownian motion conditioned
to stay positive as explained in Section
1.4.2
.
We show that the Dyson model inherits these two aspects
from BES
3

[
31
]
.

1.5.2
DYS
2
realized as
eigenvalue process of Hermitian-matrix-valued BM

Dyson
[
19
]
introduced the processes (
1.65
)
with
\beta=1,2
, and 4 as the eigenvalue processes
of matrix-valued stochastic processes
in order to realize the point processes in equilibrium called
the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble (GOE),
the Gaussian unitary ensemble (GUE),
and the Gaussian symplectic ensemble (GSE),
which are extensively studied in

random matrix theory

[
53
,
21
,
2
,
1
]
.

For
\beta=2
with given
N\in\mathbb{N}
, we prepare

N
-tuples of BMs

\{B_{ii}^{x_{i}}(t)\}_{i=1}^{N}
,
t\geq 0
,
each of which starts from
x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, and

N(N-1)/2
-tuples of pairs of BMs

\{B_{ij}(t),\widetilde{B}_{ij}(t)\}_{1\leq i<j\leq N}
,
t\geq 0
,
starting from the origin.
Here, there is a total of
N+2\times N(N-1)/2=N^{2}
BMs,
each of them independent from the rest.
Then consider an
N\times N

Hermitian-matrix-valued Brownian motion
,

{\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}B_{11}^{x_{1}}(t)&\displaystyle{\frac{B_{12}(t)+\sqrt{-1}\widetilde{B}_{12}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}}&\cdots&\displaystyle{\frac{B_{1N}(t)+\sqrt{-1}\widetilde{B}_{1N}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}}\cr\displaystyle{\frac{B_{12}(t)-\sqrt{-1}\widetilde{B}_{12}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}}&B_{22}^{x_{2}}(t)&\cdots&\displaystyle{\frac{B_{2N}(t)+\sqrt{-1}\widetilde{B}_{2N}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}}\cr\cdots&\cdots&\cdots&\cdots\cr\displaystyle{\frac{B_{1N}(t)-\sqrt{-1}\widetilde{B}_{1N}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}}&\displaystyle{\frac{B_{2N}(t)-\sqrt{-1}\widetilde{B}_{2N}(t)}{\sqrt{2}}}&\cdots&B_{NN}^{x_{N}}(t)\end{array}\right).

(1.67)

Remember that when we introduced BES
D
in Section
1.4
,
we considered the
D
-dimensional vector-valued Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
(
1.41
), by preparing
D
-tuples of
independent BMs for its elements.
Here we consider the space of
N\times N
Hermitian matrices
denoted by
{\cal H}(N)
.
Since the dimension of this space is

{\rm dim}\,{\cal H}(N)=N^{2}
,
we need
N^{2}
independent BMs for elements
to describe a Brownian motion in this space
{\cal H}(N)
.
Hence we can regard
the process
{\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(t),\ t\geq 0
defined by (
1.67
)
as a ‘Brownian motion in
{\cal H}(N)
’.
By definition, the initial state of this Brownian motion is
the diagonal matrix

{\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(0)={\rm diag}(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N}).

(1.68)

We assume
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\cdots\leq x_{N}
.

Corresponding to calculating the absolute value (
1.42
) of

\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)
, by which BES
D
was introduced,
here we calculate the eigenvalues of
{\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(t)
.
For any
t\geq 0
, there is a family of
N\times N

unitary matrices
\{{\sf U}(t)\}
which diagonalizes
{\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(t)
,

{\sf U}^{*}(t){\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(t){\sf U}(t)={\rm diag}(\lambda_{1}(t),\dots,\lambda_{N}(t))=:\Lambda(t),\quad t\geq 0.

Here for a matrix
{\sf M}=(M_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
,
we define its Hermitian conjugate by

{\sf M}^{*}=(\overline{M_{ji}})_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
,
where
\overline{z}
denotes the complex conjugate of
z\in\mathbb{C}
.
Consider a subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{N}
defined by

\mathbb{W}_{N}:=\{\bm{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:x_{1}<x_{2}<\cdots<x_{N}\},

(1.69)

which is called the

Weyl chamber

in representation theory.
If we impose the condition

(\lambda_{i}(t))_{i=1}^{N}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,

{\sf U}(t)
is uniquely determined at each time
t\geq 0
.

The following theorem is established.

Theorem 1.4

The

eigenvalue process

(\lambda_{i}(t))_{i=1}^{N},\,t\geq 0

of the Hermitian-matrix-valued Brownian motion (
1.67
)
started at (
1.68
) satisfies the SDEs,

d\lambda_{i}(t)=dB_{i}^{x_{i}}(t)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq N,\cr j\not=i\end{subarray}}\frac{dt}{\lambda_{i}(t)-\lambda_{j}(t)},\quad t\geq 0,\quad i=1,\dots,N,

(1.70)

where
(B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t))_{i=1}^{N},\ t\geq 0
are independent
BMs different from the
N^{2}
-tuples of BMs used to define
{\sf B}^{\bm{x}}(t)

in (
1.67
).
That is, this process realizes DYS
2
.

The correspondence
between BES
3
and DYS
2

in terms of equivalent processes
is summarized as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lccl}{\mbox{\bf[Aspect 1]}}&&&\cr&&&\quad\mbox{radial coordinate of}\cr&\mbox{BES${}_{3}$}&\Longleftrightarrow&\quad\mbox{$D=3$ vector-valued}\cr&&&\quad\mbox{Brownian motion}\cr&&&\cr&\mbox{}&&\quad\mbox{eigenvalue process of}\cr&\mbox{$N$-particle DYS${}_{2}$}&\Longleftrightarrow&\quad\mbox{$N\times N$ Hermitian-matrix-valued}\cr&&&\quad\mbox{Brownian motion}\cr\end{array}

Dyson derived (
1.70
) by applying the perturbation theory in
quantum mechanics
[
19
]
.
Since
(\lambda_{i}(t))_{i=1}^{N},\ t\geq 0

are functionals of
\{B_{ii}^{x_{i}}(t),B_{ij}(t),\widetilde{B}_{ij}(t)\}_{1\leq i<j\leq N}
,
t\geq 0
,
we can use Itô’s formula
to prove Theorem
1.4
.
A key point to prove the theorem is applying

Itô’s rule for
differentiating the product of

matrix-valued semi-martingales
:
If
{\sf X}(t)=(X_{ij}(t))
and
{\sf Y}(t)=(Y_{ij}(t))
are
N\times N
matrices
with semi-martingale elements,
then

d({\sf X}^{*}(t){\sf Y}(t))=d{\sf X}^{*}(t){\sf Y}(t)+{\sf X}^{*}(t)d{\sf Y}(t)+\langle d{\sf X}^{*},d{\sf Y}\rangle_{t},\quad t\geq 0,

(1.71)

where
\langle d{\sf X}^{*},d{\sf Y}\rangle_{t}

denotes an
N\times N
matrix-valued process,
whose
(i,j)
-th element is given by the finite-variation process

\sum_{k}\langle d\overline{X_{ki}},dY_{kj}\rangle_{t}
,

1\leq i,j\leq N
.

1.6
Determinantal martingale representation (DMR)
of DYS
2

1.6.1
General formula

Denote the
N
-dimensional Laplacian
with respect to the variables

\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
by

\Delta^{(N)}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}.

We set

h_{N}(\bm{x}):=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[x_{j}^{i-1}],

(1.72)

where the determinant appearing here is
called the

Vandermonde determinant
.
As we have seen in
1.4.2
,

h_{1}(x):=x
is a positive harmonic function in
\mathbb{R}_{+}=(0,\infty)

which satisfies the boundary condition
h_{1}(0)=0
.
Similarly, we can see that

\Delta^{(N)}h_{N}(\bm{x})=0.

(1.73)

and

h_{N}(\bm{x})>0,\,\mbox{if $\bm{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}$},\quad\mbox{and}\quad h_{N}(\bm{x})=0,\,\mbox{if $\bm{x}\in\partial\mathbb{W}_{N}$}.

(1.74)

We have another correspondence between BES
3
and
DYS
2
.

\begin{array}[]{lccl}{\mbox{\bf[Aspect 2]}}&&&\cr&\mbox{BES${}_{3}$}&\Longleftrightarrow&\quad\mbox{$h$-transformation by $h_{1}$ of absorbing BM in $\mathbb{R}_{+}$}\cr&&\Longleftrightarrow&\quad\mbox{BM conditioned to stay positive}\cr&&&\cr&\mbox{$N$-particle DYS${}_{2}$}&\Longleftrightarrow&\quad\mbox{$h$-transformation by $h_{N}$ of absorbing BM
in $\mathbb{W}_{N}$}\cr&&\Longleftrightarrow&\quad\mbox{noncolliding BM}\end{array}

As
[Aspect 2]
, the Dyson model is constructed as the

h
-transformation of the absorbing Brownian motion in
\mathbb{W}_{N}
.
Therefore, at any positive time
t>0
the configuration
is an element of
\mathbb{W}_{N}
,

\bm{X}(t)=(X_{1}(t),X_{2}(t),\dots,X_{N}(t))\in\mathbb{W}_{N}\quad t>0,

(1.75)

and hence there are no multiple points at which
coincidence of particle positions,

X_{i}(t)=X_{j}(t),i\not=j
, occurs.
We can consider, however, the Dyson model
starting from initial configurations with multiple points.
In order to describe configurations with multiple points,
we represent each particle configuration by a sum
of delta measures in the form

\xi(\cdot)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\delta_{x_{i}}(\cdot)

(1.76)

with a sequence of points in
\mathbb{R},\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
,
where
\mathbb{I}
is a countable index set.
Here for
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\delta_{y}(\cdot)
denotes the delta measure such that

\delta_{y}(\{x\})=1
for
x=y
and

\delta_{y}(\{x\})=0
otherwise.
Then, for (
1.76
) and
A\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\xi(A)=\int_{A}\xi(dx)=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}:x_{i}\in A}1=\sharp\{x_{i},x_{i}\in A\}
.

The measures of the form (
1.76
)
satisfying the condition

\xi(K)<\infty
for any compact subset
K\subset\mathbb{R}
are called the

nonnegative integer-valued Radon measures
on
\mathbb{R}

and we denote the space they form by
{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})
;

{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R}):=\left\{\xi=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\delta_{x_{i}}:x_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\xi(K)<\infty\,\,\mbox{for all bounded set $K\subset\mathbb{R}$}\right\}.

(1.77)

The set of configurations without multiple points
is denoted by

{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R}):=\{\xi\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R}):\xi(\{x\})\leq 1,^{\forall}x\in\mathbb{R}\}.

There is a trivial correspondence between
\mathbb{W}_{N}

and
{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.
We call
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
a

labeled configuration

and
\xi\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})
an

unlabeled configuration
.

We introduce a sequence of independent BMs,

\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)=(B_{i}^{x_{i}}(t))_{i\in\mathbb{I}},\ t\geq 0
,
in
(\Omega,{\cal F},{\rm P}^{\bm{x}})
with expectation
written as
{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}
.

In this section we assume that

\xi=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
,

\xi(\mathbb{R})=N\in\mathbb{N}

and consider DYS
2
as an
{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
-valued
diffusion process,

\Xi(t,\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i}(t)}(\cdot),\quad t\geq 0,

(1.78)

starting from the initial configuration
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
,
where
\bm{X}(t)=(X_{1}(t),\cdots,X_{N}(t))
is the
solution of (
1.65
) with
\beta=2
under the initial
configuration
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
.
We write the process as
(\Xi,\mathbb{P}^{\xi})

and express the expectation with respect to
the probability law
\mathbb{P}^{\xi}
of the Dyson model by

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}[\,\cdot\,]
.
We introduce a filtration
\{({\cal F}_{\Xi})_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\infty)}

on the space of continuous paths

{\rm C}([0,\infty)\to{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R}))
defined by

({\cal F}_{\Xi})_{t}=\sigma(\Xi(s),s\in[0,t])
, where

\sigma
denotes the smallest
\sigma
-field.

[Aspect 2]
of the Dyson model is expressed by
the following equality:
for any
({\cal F}_{\Xi})_{t}
-measurable bounded function
F
,

0\leq t\leq T<\infty
,

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}[F(\Xi(\cdot))]={\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[F\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{B_{i}(\cdot)}\right){\bf 1}_{(\tau>T)}\frac{h_{N}(\bm{B}(T))}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\right],

(1.79)

where

\tau:=\inf\{t>0:\bm{B}^{\bm{x}}(t)\notin\mathbb{W}_{N}\}

(1.80)

and
we have assumed the relations

\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}

and (
1.78
).

In the following lemma, we claim that
even if we delete the indicator
{\bf 1}_{(\tau>T)}

in RHS of (
1.79
),
still the equality holds.
It is a multivariate extension of the claim by which
we replaced (
1.58
) by (
1.59
)
in Section
1.4.2
.

Lemma 1.5

Assume that
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
,

\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
.
For any
({\cal F}_{\Xi})_{t}
-measurable bounded function
F
,

0\leq t\leq T<\infty
,

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}[F(\Xi(\cdot))]={\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[F\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{B_{i}(\cdot)}\right)\frac{h_{N}(\bm{B}(T))}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\right].

(1.81)

In Section
1.3
, we introduced
the fundamental martingale polynomials
associated with BM,
\{m_{n}(t,x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.
Since they are monic polynomials, we can prove the equalities

\displaystyle\frac{h_{N}(\bm{y})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[y_{j}^{i-1}]

(1.82)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[m_{i-1}(t,y_{j})]

for an arbitrary
t\in[0,\infty)
.
This implies that
(h_{N}(\bm{B}(t))/h_{N}(\bm{x}))_{t\geq 0}
is a martingale.

Here we extend the integral transformation
defined by (
1.39
) with (
1.36
) in
Section
1.3
to a linear integral transformation
of multivariate functions as follows.
When
F^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}^{(i)}(x_{j})
,
i=1,2

are given for
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then we define

{\cal I}\left[F^{(i)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\right]:=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\cal I}\left[\left.f^{(i)}_{j}(W_{j})\right|(t_{j},x_{j})\right],\quad i=1,2,

and

\displaystyle{\cal I}\Big[c_{1}F^{(1)}({\bf W})+c_{2}F^{(2)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\Big]

\displaystyle\quad:=c_{1}{\cal I}\Big[F^{(1)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\Big]+c_{2}{\cal I}\Big[F^{(2)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\Big],

c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
,
for
0<t_{i}<\infty,i=1,\dots,N
,
where
{\bf W}=(W_{1},\dots,W_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
In particular, if
t_{\ell}=t,1\leq{{}^{\forall}\ell}\leq N
, we write

{\cal I}[\,\cdot\,|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}]
simply as

{\cal I}[\,\cdot\,|(t,\bm{x})]
with
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.
Then (
1.82
) is further rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{h_{N}(\bm{y})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\Big[{\cal I}[(W_{j})^{i-1}|(t,y_{j})]\Big]

(1.83)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal I}\left[\left.\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[(W_{j})^{i-1}]\right|(t,\bm{y})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal I}\left[\left.\frac{h_{N}({\bf W})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\right|(t,\bm{y})\right],

where the multilinearity of determinants has been used.

Now we use the following determinant identity.

Lemma 1.6

For
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
,

\bm{z}=(z_{1},\dots,z_{N})\in\mathbb{C}^{N}
,

\frac{h_{N}(\bm{z})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\Big[\Phi_{\xi}^{x_{i}}(z_{j})\Big],

(1.84)

where

\Phi_{\xi}^{u}(z)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq N,\cr x_{k}\not=u\end{subarray}}\frac{z-x_{k}}{u-x_{k}},\quad\mbox{for $\displaystyle{\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),z,u\in\mathbb{C}}$}.

(1.85)

Then (
1.83
) is written as

\displaystyle\frac{h_{N}(\bm{y})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal I}\left[\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[\left.\Phi_{\xi}^{x_{i}}(W_{j})]\right|(t,\bm{y})\right]

(1.86)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[{\cal M}_{\xi}^{x_{i}}(t,y_{j})],

where

{\cal M}_{\xi}^{x}(t,y):={\cal I}[\Phi_{\xi}^{x}(W)|(t,y)],\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0.

(1.87)

Proposition 1.7

Assume that
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.
The following are satisfied by (
1.87
).

(i)

({\cal M}_{\xi}^{x_{i}}(t,B(t)))_{t\geq 0},i=1,\dots N

are continuous martingales.

(ii)   For any time
t\geq 0
,
{\cal M}_{\xi}^{x_{i}}(t,y),i=1,\dots,N

are linearly independent functions of
y
.

(iii)   For
1\leq i,j\leq N
,

\lim_{t\downarrow 0}{\rm E}^{x_{i}}[{\cal M}_{\xi}^{x_{j}}(t,B(t))]=\delta_{ij}
.

Then for any
({\cal F}_{\Xi})_{t}
-measurable bounded function
F
,

0\leq t\leq T<\infty
, the equality

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}[F(\Xi(\cdot))]={\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[F\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{B_{i}(\cdot)}\right){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))\right]

(1.88)

holds, where

{\cal D}_{\xi}(t,\bm{y})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[{\cal M}_{\xi}^{y_{j}}(t,y_{i})],\quad\bm{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{N})\in\mathbb{W}_{N},\quad t\geq 0.

(1.89)

We remark that
{\cal D}_{\xi}(t,\bm{B}(t)),\ t\geq 0
is indeed a
continuous martingale by part (i) and
is not identically zero by part (ii) of Proposition
1.7
.
We call
{\cal D}_{\xi}(t,\bm{B}(t)),\ t\geq 0
a

determinantal martingale

and the equality (
1.88
)
the

determinantal martingale representation
(DMR)
of DYS
2

[
30
]
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, an index set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}

is denoted by
\mathbb{I}_{n}
.
Fixing
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N^{\prime}\in\mathbb{I}_{N}
,
we write
\mathbb{J}\subset\mathbb{I}_{N},\sharp\mathbb{J}=N^{\prime}
,
if
\mathbb{J}=\{j_{1},\dots,j_{N^{\prime}}\},1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{N^{\prime}}\leq N
.
For
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
put
\bm{x}_{\mathbb{J}}=(x_{j_{1}},\dots,x_{j_{N^{\prime}}})
.
In particular, we write

\bm{x}_{N^{\prime}}=\bm{x}_{\mathbb{I}_{N^{\prime}}},1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
.
(By definition
\bm{x}_{N}=\bm{x}
.)
A collection of all permutations of
elements in
\mathbb{J}
is denoted by
\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{J})
.
In particular, we write
\mathbb{S}_{N^{\prime}}=\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{I}_{N^{\prime}}),1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
.

The following shows the

reducibility
of the determinantal martingale
in the sense that,
if we observe a symmetric function depending
on
N^{\prime}
variables,
N^{\prime}\leq N
,
then the size of determinantal
martingale can be reduced from
N
to
N^{\prime}
.

Lemma 1.8

Assume that

\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{I}_{N}}\delta_{x_{i}}

with
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}
.
Let
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
.
For
0<t\leq T<\infty
and an

({\cal F}_{\Xi})_{t}
-measurable symmetric function

F_{N^{\prime}}
on
\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbb{J}\subset\mathbb{I}_{N},\sharp\mathbb{J}=N^{\prime}}{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[F_{N^{\prime}}(\bm{B}_{\mathbb{J}}(t)){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{W}_{N^{\prime}}}\xi^{\otimes N^{\prime}}(d\bm{v}){\rm E}^{\bm{v}}\left[F_{N^{\prime}}(\bm{B}_{N^{\prime}}(t)){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}_{N^{\prime}}(T))\right].

(1.90)

1.6.2
Time-dependent density function
\rho_{\xi}(t,x)

The

density function
at a single time for

(\Xi,\mathbb{P}^{\xi}),\xi\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
is denoted by

\rho_{\xi}(t,x)
.
It is defined as a continuous function of
x\in\mathbb{R}
for

0\leq t\leq T<\infty
such that for any

test function
,

\chi\in{\rm C}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R})
,

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(x)\Xi(t,dx)\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx\,\chi(x)\rho_{\xi}(t,x).

(1.91)

The test function
\chi
is symmetrized as

\displaystyle{g(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\chi(x_{i})}
,
which is applied as
F
to the DMR (
1.88
),
and we obtain the equality

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\chi(X_{i}(t))\right]={\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\chi(B_{i}(t)){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))\right],\quad 0\leq t\leq T<\infty.

(1.92)

The LHS of (
1.92
) gives

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\chi(X_{i}(t))\right]=\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(x)\Xi(t,dx)\right]

by (
1.78
).
On the other hand, RHS of (
1.92
)
is reduced by Lemma
1.8
as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}[\chi(B_{i}(t)){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(dv){\rm E}^{v}[\chi(B(t)){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}(t,B(t))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(dv)\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx\,\chi(x)p(t,x|v){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}(t,x).

By Fubini’s theorem, we can rewrite it as

\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx\,\chi(x){\cal G}_{\xi}(t,x;t,x),

where

{\cal G}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(dv)p(s,x|v){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}(t,y).

(1.93)

Then (
1.91
) gives

\rho_{\xi}(t,x)={\cal G}_{\xi}(t,x;t,x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\quad t\geq 0.

(1.94)

1.6.3
Two-time correlation function
\rho_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)

For
0\leq t_{1}<t_{2}\leq T<\infty
, set

g_{1}(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\chi_{1}(x_{i}),\quad g_{2}(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\chi_{2}(x_{i}),

where
\chi_{m}\in{\rm C}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}),m=1,2
, and put

F(\Xi(\cdot))=\prod_{m=1}^{2}g_{m}(\bm{X}(t_{m})).

If we apply this to DMR, (
1.88
),
we obtain the equality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\chi_{1}(X_{i}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(X_{j}(t_{2}))\right]

\displaystyle\quad={\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\chi_{1}(B_{i}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B_{j}(t_{2})){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))\right]\hskip-2.84544pt,\,0\leq t\leq T<\infty.

(1.95)

The LHS of (
1.95
)
defines the two-time correlation function

\rho_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)
as

\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\chi_{1}(X_{i}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(X_{j}(t_{2}))\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\chi_{1}(x_{1})\chi_{2}(x_{2})\rho_{\xi}(t_{1},x_{1};t_{2},x_{2}).

(1.96)

On the other hand, RHS of (
1.95
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}[\chi_{1}(B_{i}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B_{j}(t_{2})){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq N,\cr i\not=j\end{subarray}}{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}[\chi_{1}(B_{i}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B_{j}(t_{2})){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))]

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{1\leq i\leq N}{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}[\chi_{1}(B_{i}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B_{i}(t_{2})){\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))].

By the reducibility of DMR given by Lemma
1.8
,
the last expression becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\xi^{\otimes 2}(d\bm{v})

\displaystyle\quad\times\ {\rm E}^{(v_{1},v_{2})}\left[\chi_{1}(B_{1}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B_{2}(t_{2}))\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{1}}(T,B_{1}(T))&{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{1}}(T,B_{2}(T))\cr{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{2}}(T,B_{1}(T))&{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{2}}(T,B_{2}(T))\end{array}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(dv){\rm E}^{v}[\chi_{1}(B(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B(t_{2})){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}(T,B(T))]

If we use the martingale property (i) of
{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}

in Proposition
1.7
, it is written as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\xi^{\otimes 2}(d\bm{v})

\displaystyle\quad\times\ {\rm E}^{(v_{1},v_{2})}\left[\chi_{1}(B_{1}(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B_{2}(t_{2}))\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{1}}(t_{1},B_{1}(t_{1}))&{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{1}}(t_{2},B_{2}(t_{2}))\cr{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{2}}(t_{1},B_{1}(t_{1}))&{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v_{2}}(t_{2},B_{2}(t_{2}))\end{array}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(dv){\rm E}^{v}[\chi_{1}(B(t_{1}))\chi_{2}(B(t_{2})){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}(t_{2},B(t_{2}))].

By Fubini’s theorem, this is equal to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\chi_{1}(x_{1})\chi_{2}(x_{2})\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{1},x_{1};t_{1},x_{1})&{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{1},x_{1};t_{2},x_{2})\cr{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{2},x_{2};t_{1},x_{1})&{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{2},x_{2};t_{2},x_{2})\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\chi_{1}(x_{1})\chi_{2}(x_{2}){\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{1},x_{1};t_{2},x_{2})p(t_{2}-t_{1},x_{2}|x_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}dx_{1}dx_{2}\,\chi_{1}(x_{1})\chi_{2}(x_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{1},x_{1};t_{1},x_{1})&{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{1},x_{1};t_{2},x_{2})\cr{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{2},x_{2};t_{1},x_{1})-p(t_{2}-t_{1},x_{2}|x_{1})&{\cal G}_{\xi}(t_{2},x_{2};t_{2},x_{2})\end{array}\right).

Since this is equal to (
1.96
),
the two-time correlation function is determined as

\rho_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)=\det\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(s,x;s,x)&\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)\cr\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(t,y;s,x)&\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(t,y;t,y)\end{array}\right)

(1.109)

for
0\leq s<t<\infty
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
where

\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)={\cal G}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)-{\bf 1}_{(s>t)}p(s-t,x|y).

(1.110)

1.7
Determinantal stochastic processes (DSPs)

1.7.1
From DMR to DSP

In the previous section, the density function
at a single time
\rho_{\xi}(t,x)
and the two-time
(and two-point) correlation function

\rho_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)
were defined by
(
1.91
) and (
1.96
), respectively.
In order to give a general definition of

spatio-temporal correlation functions

here we consider the

Laplace transformations of
the multitime joint distribution functions
of
(\Xi,\mathbb{P}^{\xi})
.
For any integer
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
a sequence of times

\bm{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{M})\in[0,\infty)^{M}
with

0\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{M}<\infty
,
and a sequence of functions

\bm{f}=(f_{t_{1}},\dots,f_{t_{M}})\in{\rm C}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R})^{M}
,
let

\Psi_{\xi}^{\bm{t}}[\bm{f}]\equiv\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\exp\left\{\sum_{m=1}^{M}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f_{t_{m}}(x)\Xi(t_{m},dx)\right\}\right].

(1.111)

By (
1.78
), if we set
test functions as

\chi_{t_{m}}(\cdot)=e^{f_{t_{m}}(\cdot)}-1,\quad 1\leq m\leq M,

(1.112)

we can rewrite (
1.111
) in the form

\Psi_{\xi}^{\bm{t}}[\bm{f}]=\mathbb{E}^{\xi}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\{1+\chi_{t_{m}}(X_{i}(t_{m}))\}\right].

(1.113)

We expand this with respect to test functions
and define the spatio-temporal correlation functions

\{\rho_{\xi}\}
as coefficients,

\Psi_{\xi}^{\bm{t}}[\bm{f}]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq N_{m}\leq N,\\
1\leq m\leq M\end{subarray}}\int_{\prod_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{W}_{N_{m}^{\rm A}}}\prod_{m=1}^{M}d\bm{x}_{N_{m}}^{(m)}\prod_{i=1}^{N_{m}}\chi_{t_{m}}\Big(x_{i}^{(m)}\Big)\rho_{\xi}\Big(t_{1},\bm{x}^{(1)}_{N_{1}};\dots;t_{M},\bm{x}^{(M)}_{N_{M}}\Big),

(1.114)

where
\bm{x}^{(m)}_{N_{m}}
denotes

(x^{(m)}_{1},\dots,x^{(m)}_{N_{m}})

and

d\bm{x}^{(m)}_{N_{m}}=\prod_{i=1}^{N_{m}}dx^{(m)}_{i}
,

1\leq m\leq M
.
Here the empty products equal 1 by convention
and the term with
N_{m}=0,1\leq\forall m\leq M

is considered to be 1.
The previous two examples
\rho_{\xi}(t,x)
and

\rho_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)
are special cases in which we set

M=1,\ t_{1}=t,\ N_{1}=1,\ x_{1}^{(1)}=x
, and

M=2,\ t_{1}=s,\ t_{2}=t,\ N_{1}=N_{2}=1,\ x_{1}^{(1)}=x,\ x_{1}^{(2)}=y
,
respectively.
The function
\Psi_{\xi}^{\bm{t}}[\bm{f}]
is a

generating function of correlation functions
.

Given an integral kernel,

{\bf K}(s,x;t,y)
,

(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,
and a sequence of functions

(\chi_{t_{1}},\dots,\chi_{t_{M}})\in{\rm C}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R})^{M},\ M\in\mathbb{N}
,
the

Fredholm determinant
associated with
{\bf K}

and
(\chi_{t_{m}})_{m=1}^{M}
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm Det}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,t)\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{M}\}^{2},\\
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{subarray}}\Big[\delta_{st}\delta_{x}(\{y\})+{\bf K}(s,x;t,y)\chi_{t}(y)\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq N_{m}\leq N,\\
1\leq m\leq M\end{subarray}}\int_{\prod_{m=1}^{M}\mathbb{W}_{N_{m}}^{\rm A}}\prod_{m=1}^{M}d\bm{x}_{N_{m}}^{(m)}\prod_{k=1}^{N_{m}}\chi_{t_{m}}\Big(x_{k}^{(m)}\Big)\det_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N_{m},1\leq j\leq N_{n},\\
1\leq m,n\leq M\end{subarray}}\Bigg[{\bf K}(t_{m},x_{i}^{(m)};t_{n},x_{j}^{(n)})\Bigg].

(1.115)

Definition 1.9

If any moment generating function (
1.111
)
is given by a Fredholm determinant,
the process
(\Xi,\mathbb{P}^{\xi})
is said to be a

determinantal stochastic process
(DSP).
In this case
all spatio-temporal correlation functions
are given by determinants as

\rho_{\xi}\Big(t_{1},\bm{x}^{(1)}_{N_{1}};\dots;t_{M},\bm{x}^{(M)}_{N_{M}}\Big)=\det_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq N_{m},1\leq j\leq N_{n},\\
1\leq m,n\leq M\end{subarray}}\Bigg[\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(t_{m},x_{i}^{(m)};t_{n},x_{j}^{(n)})\Bigg],

(1.116)

0\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{M}<\infty
,

1\leq N_{m}\leq N
,

\bm{x}^{(m)}_{N_{m}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{m}},1\leq m\leq M\in\mathbb{N}
.
Here the integral kernel,
\mathbb{K}_{\xi}:([0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R})^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
,
is a function of the initial configuration

\xi
and is called
the

correlation kernel
.

Remark 1.10

If the process
(\Xi,\mathbb{P}^{\xi})
is DSP,
then, at each time
0\leq t<\infty
,
all spatial correlation functions are
given by determinants as

\rho_{\xi}(\bm{x}_{N^{\prime}})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N^{\prime}}[{\rm K}(x_{i},x_{j})],\quad 1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N,

(1.117)

with
{\rm K}(x,y)=\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(t,x;t,y)
.
In general a random integer-valued
Radon measure in
{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})
(resp.
{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
) is called a

point process

(resp.
simple point process
).
A simple point process is said to be a

determinantal point process
(DPP)
(or

Fermion point process
)
with kernel
{\rm K}
,
if its spatial correlation functions exist
and are given in the form (
1.117
).
When
{\rm K}
is symmetric,

i.e.
,

{\rm K}(x,y)={\rm K}(y,x),\ x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,
Soshnikov
[
81
]

and Shirai and Takahashi
[
76
,
77
,
78
]

gave sufficient
conditions for
{\rm K}
to be a correlation kernel
of a determinantal point process.
See also
[
35
]
.
The notion of DSP
given by Definition
1.9

is a dynamical extension of
the determinantal point process
[
10
,
38
]
.

Let
\Xi
and
\widetilde{\Xi}

be determinantal processes with
correlation kernels
\mathbb{K}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{K}}
,
respectively.
If there is a function
G(s,x)
, which is continuous
with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}
for any fixed
s\in[0,\infty)
,
such that

\mathbb{K}(s,x;t,y)=\frac{G(s,x)}{G(t,y)}\widetilde{\mathbb{K}}(s,x;t,y),\quad(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

(1.118)

then we say that

\widetilde{\Xi}
is a

gauge transform
of
\Xi
.

Lemma 1.11

Under (
1.118
),

(\Xi(t))_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm(law)}}{{=}}(\widetilde{\Xi}(t))_{t\geq 0}.

In other words, DSP is
gauge invariant
.

By Proposition
1.7
,
(
1.113
) has the DMR

\Psi_{\xi}^{\bm{t}}[\bm{f}]={\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\{1+\chi_{t_{m}}(B_{i}(t_{m}))\}{\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))\right],

(1.119)

where
T\geq t_{M}
and
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
.
The following equality is established
[
30
]
.

Lemma 1.12

Let
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{W}_{N}^{\rm A}
and

\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
.
Then for any
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
0\leq t_{1}<\cdots<t_{M}\leq T<\infty
,

\chi_{t_{m}}\in{\rm C}_{\rm c}(\mathbb{R}),1\leq m\leq M
,
the equality

\displaystyle{\rm E}^{\bm{x}}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{M}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\{1+\chi_{t_{m}}(B_{i}(t_{m}))\}{\cal D}_{\xi}(T,\bm{B}(T))\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathop{{\rm Det}}_{\begin{subarray}{c}(s,t)\in\{t_{1},\dots,t_{M}\}^{2},\\
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{subarray}}\Big[\delta_{st}\delta_{x}(\{y\})+\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)\chi_{t}(y)\Big]

(1.120)

holds,
where
\mathbb{K}_{\xi}
is given by (
1.110
)
with (
1.93
).

Now we arrive at the following full characterization of
DYS
2
,
(\Xi,\mathbb{P}^{\xi})
.

Theorem 1.13

For any finite and fixed initial configuration without multiple points,
that is, for
\xi\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),\xi(\mathbb{R})=N\in\mathbb{N}
,
DYS
2
is determinantal.
Its correlation kernel is given by

\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)={\cal G}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)-{\bf 1}_{(s>t)}p(s-t,x|y),\quad(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}

(1.121)

with

{\cal G}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi(dv)p(s,x|v){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}(t,y).

(1.122)

1.7.2
Generalization for initial configuration
with multiple points

For general
\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})

with
\xi(\mathbb{R})=N<\infty
, define

{\rm supp}\,\xi=\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\xi(x)>0\}
and let

\xi_{*}(\cdot)=\sum_{v\in{\rm supp}\,\xi}\delta_{v}(\cdot).

For
s\in[0,\infty)
,
v,x\in\mathbb{R}
,
z,\zeta\in\mathbb{C}
, let

\phi_{\xi}^{v}((s,x);z,\zeta)=\frac{p(s,x|\zeta)}{p(s,x|v)}\frac{1}{z-\zeta}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{z-x_{i}}{\zeta-x_{i}},

(1.123)

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{\xi}^{v}((s,x);z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{C(\delta_{v})}d\zeta\,\phi^{v}_{\xi}((s,x);z,\zeta)

(1.124)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Res}\,\Big[\phi^{v}_{\xi}((s,x);z,\zeta);\zeta=v\Big],

where

C(\delta_{v})
is a closed contour on the complex plane
\mathbb{C}

encircling the point
v

once in the positive direction.
This function (
1.124
) is entire with respect to
z\in\mathbb{C}

parameterized by
(s,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}

in addition to
v\in\mathbb{C},\xi\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})
.
Remark that the polynomial function

\Phi_{\xi}^{u}(z)
defined by (
1.85
) is parameterized
only by
u\in\mathbb{C}
and
\xi\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.
Here we start from this entire function and consider
its
{\cal I}
-transformation,

{\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}((s,x)|(t,y))={\cal I}\left[\Phi_{\xi}^{v}((s,x);W)\Big|(t,y)\right],\quad(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R},

(1.125)

which provides a martingale, if we put

y=B(t),\ t\geq 0
.
Then it is easy to see that (
1.121
) with (
1.122
)
is rewritten as

\mathbb{K}_{\xi}(s,x;t,y)=\int_{S}\xi_{*}(dv)p(s,x|v){\cal M}_{\xi}^{v}((s,x)|(t,y))-{\bf 1}(s>t)p(s-t,x|y),

(1.126)

(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
.

We note that
the kernel (
1.126
) with (
1.125
)
is bounded and integrable also for
\xi\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.
Therefore, the spatio-temporal correlations are given
by (
1.116
) for any
0\leq t_{1}<\dots<t_{M}<\infty,M\in\mathbb{N}

and the finite-dimensional distributions
are determined.

Proposition 1.14

Also for
\xi\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
,
the DSPs
with the correlation kernels (
1.126
)
are well-defined.

The complete proof of this proposition was given
in Section 4.1 of
[
39
]
.
See also
[
60
,
61
,
83
,
62
]
.
The above extension will provide the

entrance laws
for
the processes
(\Xi(t),t>0,\mathbb{P}^{\xi})

in the sense of Section XII.4 in
[
64
]
.

1.7.3
DSP with extended Hermitian kernel

In order to give an example
of Proposition
1.14
,
here we study the extreme case where
all
N
points are concentrated on an origin,

\xi=N\delta_{0}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\xi_{*}=\delta_{0}\quad\mbox{with}\quad\xi(\{0\})=N.

(1.127)

For (
1.127
), (
1.123
) and (
1.124
)
become

\displaystyle\phi_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x);z,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p(s,x|\zeta)}{p(s,x|0)}\frac{1}{z-\zeta}\left(\frac{z}{\zeta}\right)^{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{p(s,x|\zeta)}{p(s,x|0)}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{N-\ell-1}}{\zeta^{N-\ell}},

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x);z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p(s,x|0)}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}z^{N-\ell-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{C(\delta_{0})}d\zeta\,\frac{p(s,x|\zeta)}{\zeta^{N-\ell}}

(1.128)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p(s,x|0)}\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}z^{N-\ell-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{C(\delta_{0})}d\zeta\,\frac{p(s,x|\zeta)}{\zeta^{N-\ell}},

since the integrands are holomorphic when
\ell\geq N
.

For BM with the transition probability density (
1.1
),
(
1.128
) gives

\displaystyle\Phi_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x);z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}z^{N-\ell-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{C(\delta_{0})}d\zeta\,\frac{e^{x\zeta/s-\zeta^{2}/2s}}{\zeta^{N-\ell}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)^{N-\ell-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{-1}}\oint_{C(\delta_{0})}d\eta\,\frac{e^{2(x/\sqrt{2s})\eta-\eta^{2}}}{\eta^{N-\ell}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{z}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)^{N-\ell-1}\frac{1}{(N-\ell-1)!}H_{N-\ell-1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right),

where we have used the contour integral representation
of the Hermite polynomials (
1.34
).
Thus its integral transformation is calculated as

\displaystyle{\cal I}\left[\left.\Phi_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x);W)\right|(t,y)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{(N-\ell-1)!}H_{N-\ell-1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\frac{1}{(2s)^{(N-\ell-1)/2}}{\cal I}[W^{N-\ell-1}|(t,y)]

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{(N-\ell-1)!}H_{N-\ell-1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\frac{1}{(2s)^{(N-\ell-1)/2}}m_{N-\ell-1}(t,y)

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{(N-\ell-1)!2^{N-\ell-1}}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{(N-\ell-1)/2}H_{N-\ell-1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)H_{N-\ell-1}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right),

where we have used Lemma
1.1
and (
1.40
)
in Section
1.3
.
Then we obtain the following,

\displaystyle{\cal M}_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x)|(t,B(t)))=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{n!2^{n}}m_{n}(s,x)m_{n}(t,B(t))

\displaystyle\qquad\quad=\sqrt{\pi}e^{x^{2}/4s+B(t)^{2}/4t}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{B(t)}{\sqrt{2t}}\right),

(1.129)

where

\varphi_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sqrt{\pi}2^{n}n!}}H_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}/2},\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(1.130)

are the

Hermite orthonormal functions
on
\mathbb{R}
,
