\int_{\mathbb{R}}dx\,\varphi_{n}(x)\varphi_{m}(x)=\delta_{nm},\quad n,m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(1.131)

The following expression for the transition probability density (
1.1
)
of BM is known as

Mehler’s formula
,

p(s-t,x|y)=\frac{e^{-x^{2}/4s}}{e^{-y^{2}/4t}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right).

(1.132)

Since
m_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
are
the fundamental martingale polynomials
associated with BM,
the process (
1.129
) is
a continuous martingale.
Then we see that

{\rm E}\left[{\cal M}_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x)|(t,\bm{B}(t)))\right]={\rm E}\left[{\cal M}_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x)|(0,\bm{B}(0)))\right]=1

for
(s,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,
0\leq t<\infty
.

By the formula (
1.126
), we obtain the
correlation kernels as

\displaystyle\mathbb{K}_{N\delta_{0}}(s,x;t,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p(s,x|0){\cal M}_{N\delta_{0}}^{0}((s,x)|(t,y))-{\bf 1}(s>t)p(s-t,x|y)

(1.133)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{e^{-x^{2}/4s}}{e^{-y^{2}/4t}}{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}(s,x;t,y)

with

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}(s,x;t,y)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\ {\bf 1}(s>t)\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right).

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)}&\quad\mbox{for $s\leq t$},\cr\displaystyle{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)}&\quad\mbox{for $s>t$},\end{array}\right.

where Mehler’s formula (
1.132
) was used.
By the
gauge invariance
(Lemma
1.11
),
the factor
e^{-x^{2}/4s}/e^{-y^{2}/4t}
in (
1.133
)
is irrelevant for DSPs.
The kernel
{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}

is known as the

extended Hermite kernel

(see, for instance, Exercise 11.6.3 in
[
21
]
).

The equal-time correlation kernel

\displaystyle{\rm K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N,\,t)}(x,y)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}(t,x;t,y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)

has the following expression,

\displaystyle K_{\rm Hermite}^{(N,\,t)}(x,y)=\sqrt{\frac{N}{2}}\,\frac{\varphi_{N}(x/\sqrt{2t})\varphi_{N-1}(y/\sqrt{2t})-\varphi_{N-1}(x/\sqrt{2t})\varphi_{N}(y/\sqrt{2t})}{x-y},

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\mbox{if $x\not=y$},

(1.137)

\displaystyle K_{\rm Hermite}^{(N,\,t)}(x,x)

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2t}}\left[N\left\{\varphi_{N}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right\}^{2}-\sqrt{N(N+1)}\varphi_{N-1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)\varphi_{N+1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)\right].

(1.138)

This spatial correlation kernel is a special case
of the

Christoffel–Dorboux kernel

(see, for instance, Chapter 9 in
[
21
]
and
Chapter 3 in
[
2
]
).
It is called
the
Hermite kernel
and defines
the determinantal point process

[
81
,
77
]
on
\mathbb{R}

such that a spatial correlation function is
given by

\rho_{\rm Hermite}^{(N,\,t)}(\bm{x}_{N^{\prime}})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N^{\prime}}\Big[{\rm K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N,\,t)}(x_{i},x_{j})\Big]

(1.139)

for any
1\leq N^{\prime}\leq N
and

\bm{x}_{N^{\prime}}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N^{\prime}})\in\mathbb{R}^{N^{\prime}}
,

t>0
.
We write the probability measure of this determinantal point
process as
{\rm P}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N,\,t)}
.

1.8
Exercises 1

1.8.1
Exercise 1.1

For
t>0
,
x\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
, let

G_{\alpha}(t,x)=e^{\alpha x-\alpha^{2}t/2}.

(1.140)

Let
(B(t))_{t\geq 0}
be the 1 dimensional standard
Brownian motion (BM) in

(\Omega,{\rm P},{\cal F})
with the filtration

{\cal F}_{t}=\sigma(B(s):0\leq s\leq t),t\geq 0
.

(1)
Prove the equality

\frac{e^{\alpha B(t)}}{{\rm E}[e^{\alpha B(t)}]}=G_{\alpha}(t,B(t)),\quad t\geq 0.

(1.141)

(2)
Prove that
(G_{\alpha}(t,B(t)))_{t\geq 0}
is
{\cal F}_{t}
-martingale;

{\rm E}[G_{\alpha}(t,B(t))|{\cal F}_{s}]=G_{\alpha}(s,B(s)),\quad 0<s<t.

(1.142)

(3)
Let
\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)
be the Fourier transformation of

G_{\alpha}(t,x)
;

\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-\sqrt{-1}\alpha w}}{2\pi}G_{\alpha}(t,x)d\alpha.

(1.143)

Show that

\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)=\frac{e^{-(\sqrt{-1}x+w)^{2}/2t}}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}.

(1.144)

(4)
Define the integral transformation by

{\cal I}[f(W)|(t,x)]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(\sqrt{-1}w)\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)dw.

(1.145)

For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\{0,1,\dots\}
, let

\displaystyle m_{n}(t,x)

\displaystyle={\cal I}[W^{n}|(t,x)]

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\sqrt{-1}w)^{n}\widehat{G}_{w}(t,x)dw,\quad t\geq 0.

(1.146)

Express
m_{n}(t,x),n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
using the
Hermite polynomials,

\displaystyle H_{n}(x)

\displaystyle:=(-1)^{n}e^{x^{2}}\frac{d^{n}e^{-x^{2}}}{dx^{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{[n/2]}(-1)^{k}\frac{n!}{k!(n-2k)!}(2x)^{n-2k}.

(1.147)

(5)
Prove the following for

\{m_{n}(t,x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
.

(i)
They are monic polynomials of degrees
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

with time-dependent coefficients:

m_{n}(t,x)=x^{n}+\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}c_{n}^{(k)}(t)x^{k},\quad t\geq 0.

(ii)
For
0\leq k\leq n-1
,
c_{n}^{(k)}(0)=0
. That is,

m_{n}(0,x)=x^{n},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(iii)   If we set
x=B(t)
, they provide martingales:

{\rm E}[m_{n}(t,B(t))|{\cal F}_{s}]=m_{n}(s,B(s)),\quad 0\leq s\leq t,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

(1.148)

1.8.2
Exercise 1.2

Let

h_{N}(\bm{x}):=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}(x_{j}-x_{i})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[x_{j}^{i-1}].

(1.149)

(1)
Using the basic properties of determinant,
verify the equalities,

\displaystyle\frac{h_{N}(\bm{y})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[y_{j}^{i-1}]

(1.150)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[m_{i-1}(t,y_{j})],

where
\{m_{n}(t,x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are given by
(
1.146
) in Exercise 1.1.

(2)
We extend the integral transformation
{\cal I}

defined by (
1.145
) in Exercise 1.1
to a linear integral transformation of multivariate functions
as follows:
When
F^{(i)}(\bm{x})=\prod_{j=1}^{N}f_{j}^{(i)}(x_{j})
,
i=1,2

are given for
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, then we define

{\cal I}\left[F^{(i)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\right]:=\prod_{j=1}^{N}{\cal I}\left[\left.f^{(i)}_{j}(W_{j})\right|(t_{j},x_{j})\right],\quad i=1,2,

(1.151)

and

\displaystyle{\cal I}\Big[c_{1}F^{(1)}({\bf W})+c_{2}F^{(2)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\Big]

\displaystyle\quad:=c_{1}{\cal I}\Big[F^{(1)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\Big]+c_{2}{\cal I}\Big[F^{(2)}({\bf W})\left|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}\right.\Big],

(1.152)

c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
,
for
0<t_{i}<\infty,i=1,\dots,N
,
where
{\bf W}=(W_{1},\dots,W_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
In particular, if
t_{\ell}=t,1\leq{{}^{\forall}\ell}\leq N
, we write

{\cal I}[\,\cdot\,|\{(t_{\ell},x_{\ell})\}_{\ell=1}^{N}]
simply as

{\cal I}[\,\cdot\,|(t,\bm{x})]
with
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})
.

Using multilinearity of determinants, verify the following
equalities,

\displaystyle\frac{h_{N}(\bm{y})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\Big[{\cal I}[(W_{j})^{i-1}|(t,y_{j})]\Big]

\displaystyle={\cal I}\left[\left.\frac{1}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}[(W_{j})^{i-1}]\right|(t,\bm{y})\right]

\displaystyle={\cal I}\left[\left.\frac{h_{N}({\bf W})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}\right|(t,\bm{y})\right].

(1.153)

(3)
Prove the equality,

\frac{h_{N}(\bm{z})}{h_{N}(\bm{x})}=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\Big[\Phi_{\xi}^{x_{i}}(z_{j})\Big],

(1.154)

where

\Phi_{\xi}^{u}(z)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq N,\cr x_{k}\not=u\end{subarray}}\frac{z-x_{k}}{u-x_{k}},

(1.155)

\xi=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R}),z,u\in\mathbb{C}
,
following the steps given below.

(i)
Let

H(\bm{x},\bm{z})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq N}\left[\prod_{1\leq k\leq N,k\not=i}(z_{j}-x_{k})\right].

(1.156)

Show that (a) it is a polynomial function of
x_{i},z_{i},1\leq i\leq N

with degree
N(N-1)
,
(b)
H(\bm{x},\bm{x})=h(\bm{x})^{2}
, and
(c)
H(\bm{x},\bm{z})=0
,
if
x_{i}=x_{j}
or
z_{i}=z_{j}
for some
i,j
with
1\leq i<j\leq N
.
It is implied by (a)–(c) that

H(\bm{x},\bm{z})=h_{N}(\bm{x})h_{N}(\bm{z})
.

(ii)
By the definition (
1.155
), hence
the RHS of (
1.154
)
is equal to

H(\bm{x},\bm{z})/h(\bm{x})^{2}

for
\xi\in{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.

1.8.3
Exercise 1.3

For a pair of space-time coordinates

(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
, consider
the function,

{\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,y))=\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\frac{1}{n!2^{n}}m_{n}(s,x)m_{n}(t,y),

(1.157)

where
\{m_{n}(t,x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are given by
(
1.146
) in Exercise 1.1.

(1)
The

Hermite orthonormal functions
are defined
by

\varphi_{n}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sqrt{\pi}2^{n}n!}}H_{n}(x)e^{-x^{2}/2},\quad x\in\mathbb{R},\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(1.158)

where
\{H_{n}(x)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
are
the Hermite polynomials defined by
(
1.147
).
Prove the following expression of
{\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,y)
,

{\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,y))=\sqrt{\pi}e^{x^{2}/4s+y^{2}/4t}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right).

(1.159)

(2)
Let
(B(t))_{t\geq 0}
be BM
adapted to the filtration

{\cal F}_{t},t\geq 0
.
Then prove that, for fixed
(s,x)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,

({\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,B(t))))_{t\geq 0}
is
an
{\cal F}_{t}
-martingale, and

{\rm E}\left[{\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,\bm{B}(t)))\right]={\rm E}\left[{\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(0,\bm{B}(0)))\right]=1.

(1.160)

(3)
The following identity is known as

Mehler’s formula
,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{H_{n}(x)H_{n}(y)}{2^{n}n!}a^{n}=\frac{e^{[2xya-(x^{2}+y^{2})a^{2}]/(1-a^{2})}}{\sqrt{1-a^{2}}},\quad|a|<1.

(1.161)

Prove the following limit,

\lim_{N\to\infty}{\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,y))=\frac{p(s-t,x|y)}{p(s,x|0)},\quad 0<t<s,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},

(1.162)

where
p(t,y|x)
denotes the
transition probability density of BM,

p(t,y|x)=\frac{e^{-(y-x)^{2}/2t}}{\sqrt{2\pi t}},\quad t>0,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.163)

(4)
Define the spatio-temporal kernel as follows,

K(s,x;t,y):=p(s,x|0){\cal M}_{N}((s,x)|(t,y))-{\bf 1}(s>t)p(s-t,x|y),

(1.164)

(s,x),(t,y)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}
,
where
{\bf 1}(\omega)
is an indicator;

{\bf 1}(\omega)=1
, if
\omega
is satisfied,
and
{\bf 1}(\omega)=0
, otherwise.
Then prove the equality

\mathbb{K}_{N\delta_{0}}(s,x;t,y)=\frac{e^{-x^{2}/4s}}{e^{-y^{2}/4t}}{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}(s,x;t,y),

(1.165)

where

\displaystyle{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}(s,x;t,y)

\displaystyle\qquad=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)}&\quad\mbox{for $s\leq t$},\cr\displaystyle{-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2s}}\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}\left(\frac{t}{s}\right)^{n/2}\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{2s}}\right)\varphi_{n}\left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{2t}}\right)}&\quad\mbox{for $s>t$}.\end{array}\right.

The spatio-temporal kernel

{\bf K}_{\rm Hermite}^{(N)}(s,x;t,y)

is known as the
extended Hermite kernel

giving the correlation kernel
for DYS
2
starting from
the configuration such that
all
N
particles are at the origin,

N\delta_{0}\in{\rm Conf}(\mathbb{R})\setminus{\rm Conf}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
.

2
Gaussian Analytic Functions and Their Zero-Point Processes

2.1
Reproducing kernel Hilbert spaces

2.1.1
Bergman spaces and Hardy spaces

A
Hilbert function space of holomorphic functions

is a Hilbert space
{\cal H}
of functions
on a domain
D
in
\mathbb{C}

equipped with the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{{\cal H}}
,

\begin{array}[]{ll}\langle af+bg,h\rangle_{{\cal H}}=a\langle f,h\rangle_{{\cal H}}+b\langle g,h\rangle_{{\cal H}},&\cr\langle h,af+bg\rangle_{{\cal H}}=\overline{a}\langle h,f\rangle_{{\cal H}}+\overline{b}\langle h,g\rangle_{{\cal H}},&\quad f,g,h\in{\cal H},\quad a,b\in\mathbb{C}\cr\langle g,f\rangle_{{\cal H}}=\overline{\langle f,g\rangle_{{\cal H}}}=\langle\overline{f},\overline{g}\rangle_{{\cal H}},&\end{array}

and the norm
\|f\|_{{\cal H}}:=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{{\cal H}}}
.
The evaluation at each point
of
D
is a continuous functional on
{\cal H}
.
Therefore, for each point
w\in D
,
there is an element of
{\cal H}
,
which is called the

reproducing kernel at
w

and denote by
k_{w}
, with the property

\langle f,k_{w}\rangle_{{\cal H}}=f(w),\quad\forall f\in{\cal H}.

Because
k_{w}\in{\cal H}
, it is itself a function on
D
,

k_{w}(z)=\langle k_{w},k_{z}\rangle_{{\cal H}}.

We write

k_{{\cal H}}(z,w):=k_{w}(z)=\langle k_{w},k_{z}\rangle_{{\cal H}}

(2.1)

and call it the
reproducing kernel
for
{\cal H}
.
By definition, it is
Hermitian
;

\overline{k_{{\cal H}}(z,w)}=k_{{\cal H}}(w,z),\quad z,w\in D.

If
{\cal H}
is a holomorphic Hilbert function space, then

k_{{\cal H}}
is holomorphic in the first variable and
anti-holomorphic in the second.
We see that
k_{{\cal H}}(z,w)
is a

positive semi-definite kernel
:
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
,
for any points
z_{i}\in D
and
\xi_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
,
i=1,2,\dots,n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{{\cal H}}(z_{i},z_{j})\xi_{i}\overline{\xi_{j}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle k_{z_{j}},k_{z_{i}}\rangle_{{\cal H}}\xi_{i}\overline{\xi_{j}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\langle k_{{\cal H}}(\cdot,z_{j}),k_{{\cal H}}(\cdot,z_{i})\rangle_{{\cal H}}\xi_{i}\overline{\xi_{j}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{{\cal H}}(z_{i},\cdot)\xi_{i},\sum_{j=1}^{n}k_{{\cal H}}(z_{j},\cdot)\xi_{j}\right\rangle_{{\cal H}}=\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}k_{{\cal H}}(z_{i},\cdot)\Big\|_{{\cal H}}^{2}\geq 0.

(2.2)

Let
\{e_{n}:n\in{\cal I}\}
be any

complete orthonormal systme

(CONS) for
{\cal H}
,
where
{\cal I}
is an index set,

\displaystyle\langle e_{n},e_{m}\rangle_{{\cal H}}=\delta_{nm},\,n,m\in{\cal I},

\displaystyle f\in{\cal H}\iff f=\sum_{n\in{\cal I}}c_{n}e_{n}\,\,\mbox{with}\,\,\,(c_{n})_{n\in{\cal I}}\in\ell^{2}({\cal I}).

Then one can prove that
the reproducing kernel for
{\cal H}
is written in the form

k_{{\cal H}}(z,w)=\sum_{n\in{\cal I}}e_{n}(z)\overline{e_{n}(w)}.

(2.3)

Actually, this gives

\displaystyle\langle f(\cdot),k_{{\cal H}}(\cdot,w)\rangle_{{\cal H}}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{m\in{\cal I}}c_{m}e_{m}(\cdot),\sum_{n\in{\cal I}}e_{n}(\cdot)\overline{e_{n}(w)}\right\rangle_{{\cal H}}=\sum_{m\in{\cal I}}\sum_{n\in{\cal I}}c_{m}\langle e_{m},e_{n}\rangle_{{\cal H}}e_{n}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in{\cal I}}c_{n}e_{n}(w)=f(w),\,\,\,\forall f\in{\cal H},\,w\in D.

We note that the positive definiteness of the kernel
(
2.2
)
is equivalent with the situation such that,
for any points
z_{i}\in D
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
, the matrix

(k_{{\cal H}}(z_{i},z_{k}))_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
has a nonnegative determinant,

\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[k_{{\cal H}}(z_{i},z_{j})]\geq 0\quad\mbox{for any $n\in\mathbb{N}$}.

First we show two examples
of holomorphic Hilbert function spaces,
the
Bergman space
and the
Hardy space
,
for a
unit disk

\mathbb{D}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|<1\}

and the domains which are conformally
transformed from
\mathbb{D}
.

Example 2.1

The
Bergman space
on
\mathbb{D}
, denoted by
L^{2}_{{\rm B}}(\mathbb{D})
, is
the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{D}

which are square-integrable with respect to
the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}
.
The inner product for
L^{2}_{{\rm B}}(\mathbb{D})
is given by

\langle f,g\rangle_{L^{2}_{{\rm B}}(\mathbb{D})}:=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{D}}f(z)\overline{g(z)}m(dz)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{f}(n)\overline{\widehat{g}(n)}}{n+1},

where the
n
th Taylor coefficient of
f
at 0 is denoted by

\widehat{f}(n)
;

f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widehat{f}(n)z^{n}
.
Let

\widetilde{e_{n}}(z):=\sqrt{n+1}z^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Then
\{\widetilde{e_{n}}(z)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
form a
CONS for
L^{2}_{{\rm B}}(\mathbb{D})

and the reproducing kernel (
2.3
) is given by

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)

\displaystyle:=k_{L^{2}_{{\rm B}}(\mathbb{D})}(z,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(n+1)(z\overline{w})^{n}=\frac{1}{(1-z\overline{w})^{2}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

(2.4)

This kernel is called the
Bergman kernel
of
\mathbb{D}
.

Example 2.2

The
Hardy space
on
\mathbb{D}
,
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
, consists of
holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{D}
such that the Taylor coefficients
form a square-summable series;

\|f\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{D})}^{2}:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}|\widehat{f}(n)|^{2}<\infty,\quad f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D}).

For every
f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
,
the non-tangential limit

\lim_{r\uparrow 1}f(re^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})
exists
a.e. by Fatou’s theorem and we write it as

f(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})
.
It is known that
f(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})\in L^{2}(\partial\mathbb{D})
.
Then one can prove that the inner product of
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})

is given by the following
three different ways,

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{2}(\mathbb{D})}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\widehat{f}(n)\overline{\widehat{g}(n)}},\cr\displaystyle{\lim_{r\uparrow 1}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(re^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})\overline{g(re^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})}d\phi},\quad f,g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{D}),\cr\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})\overline{g(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})}d\phi},\end{cases}

(2.5)

with
\|f\|_{H^{2}(\mathbb{D})}^{2}=\langle f,f\rangle_{H^{2}(\mathbb{D})}
.
Let
\sigma
be the measure on the boundary
of
\mathbb{D}
which is the usual arc length measure.
Then the last expression of the inner product (
2.5
)
is written as

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{2}(\mathbb{D})}=(1/2\pi)\int_{\gamma_{1}}f(z)\overline{g(z)}\sigma(dz)
,
where
\gamma_{1}
is a unit circle

\{e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi}:\phi\in[0,2\pi)\}
giving the boundary
of
\mathbb{D}
.
If we set
e_{n}(z):=e^{(0,0)}_{n}(z)=z^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
then
\{e_{n}(z)\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
form
CONS for
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
. The reproducing kernel (
2.3
)
is given by

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)

\displaystyle:=k_{H^{2}(\mathbb{D})}(z,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}(z\overline{w})^{n}=\frac{1}{1-z\overline{w}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D},

(2.6)

which is called the
Szegő kernel
of
\mathbb{D}
.

Let
f:D\to\widetilde{D}
be a

conformal transformation

(the angle-preserving one-to-one map)
between two bounded domains
D,\widetilde{D}\subsetneq\mathbb{C}

with
{\cal C}^{\infty}
smooth boundary (analytic boundary).
We find an argument in Chapter 12 of
[
8
]

concluding that
the derivative of the transformation
f
denoted by

f^{\prime}
has a single valued square root on
D
.
We let
\sqrt{f^{\prime}(z)}
denote one of the
square roots of
f^{\prime}
.
The Szegő kernel and the Bergman kernel
are then transformed by
f
as

[
8
, Chapters 12, 16]

\displaystyle S_{D}(z,w)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{f^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{f^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\widetilde{D}}(f(z),f(w)),

\displaystyle K_{D}(z,w)

\displaystyle=|f^{\prime}(z)||f^{\prime}(w)|K_{\widetilde{D}}(f(z),f(w)),\quad z,w\in D.

(2.7)

Consider the special case in which

D\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
is a simply connected domain
with analytic boundary
and
\widetilde{D}=\mathbb{D}
.
For each
\alpha\in D
,

Riemann’s mapping theorem

gives a unique conformal transformation;

h_{\alpha}:D\to\mathbb{D}
conformal
such that

h_{\alpha}(\alpha)=0,\,\,h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)>0
.

(2.8)

Such
h_{\alpha}
is called the

Riemann mapping function
.
By (
2.6
),
the first equation in
(
2.7
) gives

\displaystyle S_{D}(z,w)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\mathbb{D}}(h_{\alpha}(z),h_{\alpha}(w))

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(w)}}\frac{1}{1-h_{\alpha}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)}},\quad z,w\in D.

Since
h_{\alpha}(\alpha)=0
, if we put

z=\alpha
, or
w=\alpha
, or

z=w=\alpha
in this formula,
then we readily obtain

\displaystyle S_{D}(\alpha,w)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(w)}}\frac{1}{1-0\times\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)}}=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(w)}},

\displaystyle S_{D}(z,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}},\quad S_{D}(\alpha,\alpha)=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\quad

\displaystyle\frac{S_{D}(z,\alpha)S_{D}(\alpha,w)}{S_{D}(\alpha,\alpha)}=\frac{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(w)}}\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}}}{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(\alpha)}}}=\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{h_{\alpha}^{\prime}(w)}}

Hence the above formula is written as

[
7
]
,

S_{D}(z,w)=\frac{S_{D}(z,\alpha)\overline{S_{D}(w,\alpha)}}{S_{D}(\alpha,\alpha)}\frac{1}{1-h_{\alpha}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)}},\quad z,w,\alpha\in D.

(2.9)

Similarly, we have

K_{D}(z,w)=\frac{S_{D}(z,\alpha)^{2}\overline{S_{D}(w,\alpha)^{2}}}{S_{D}(\alpha,\alpha)^{2}}\frac{1}{(1-h_{\alpha}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)})^{2}},\quad z,w,\alpha\in D.

(2.10)

Hence the following relationship is established,

\displaystyle S_{D}(z,w)^{2}

\displaystyle=K_{D}(z,w),\quad z,w\in D.

(2.11)

Here we notice that (
2.9
) is rewritten as

S_{D}(z,w)-\frac{S_{D}(z,\alpha)S_{D}(\alpha,w)}{S_{D}(\alpha,\alpha)}=S_{D}(z,w)h_{\alpha}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)},\quad z,w,\alpha\in D.

(2.12)

LHS is regarded as the reproducing
kernel for the Hilbert subspace

H^{2}_{a}(D):=\{f\in H^{2}(D):f(\alpha)=0\},

(2.13)

and we will denote such a

conditional kernel
with a deterministic zero
at
\alpha
by
S_{D}^{\alpha}(z,w)
.
Hence we obtain the formula

S_{D}^{\alpha}(z,w)=S_{D}(z,w)h_{\alpha}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)},\quad z,w,\alpha\in D.

(2.14)

Let
q\in(0,1)
be a fixed number and
we consider the
annulus

\mathbb{A}_{q}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:q<|z|<1\}.

As the third example of the
Hilbert spaces of holomorphic functions,
we consider the Hardy space on
\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

Example 2.3

The
Hardy space for
\mathbb{A}_{q}
,

H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
, is the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions
on
\mathbb{A}_{q}

equipped with the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})\overline{g(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})}d\phi+\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(qe^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})\overline{g(qe^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})}\,qd\phi,\,\,f,g\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q}).

(2.15)

A CONS of
H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})

is given by
\{e^{(q,q)}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with

e^{(q,q)}_{n}(z)=\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{1+q^{2n+1}}},\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},

and the reproducing kernel is given by
[
52
]

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{(q,q)}_{n}(z)\overline{e^{(q,q)}_{n}(w)}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(z\overline{w})^{n}}{1+q^{2n+1}}.

(2.16)

This infinite series converges absolutely for
z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}

and
is called the

Szegő kernel of
\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

The nonexistence of zero in
\mathbb{D}

of
S_{\mathbb{D}}(\cdot,\alpha),\alpha\in\mathbb{D}
and the
uniqueness of zero in
\mathbb{A}_{q}
of
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\cdot,\alpha)
,

\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
are concluded from a general consideration
(see, for instance,
[
8
, Section 27]
).
Define

\widehat{\alpha}:=-\frac{q}{\overline{\alpha}},\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.17)

The fact

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\widehat{\alpha},\alpha)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,\widehat{\alpha})=0,\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}

(2.18)

was proved
as Theorem 1 in
[
82
]
by direct calculation,
for which a simpler proof can be given
using theta functions (see Lemma
2.5
below).

Again we consider the conformal transformation

f:D\to\widetilde{D}
between two bounded domains
D,\widetilde{D}\subsetneq\mathbb{C}

with analytic boundaries.
Here we consider the case in which

D
is a
2-connected domain
and
\widetilde{D}=\mathbb{D}
.
For each
\alpha\in D
,
there exists a unique function

f_{a}
giving
a
branched 2 to 1 covering map
of
D
to
\mathbb{D}
,
in which a unique point

\widehat{\alpha}=\widehat{\alpha}(\alpha)\not=\alpha
,

\widehat{\alpha}\in D
exists
such that

f_{\alpha}(\alpha)=f_{\alpha}(\widehat{\alpha})=0.

The map
f_{\alpha},\alpha\in D

is called the
Ahlfors mapping function

[
8
, Chapter 13]
.
By the fact (
2.18
) with (
2.17
),
for an arbitrary, but fixed
\alpha\in D
,
we can verify that the following set forms
a CONS for
H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
which is different
from
\{e^{(q,q)}_{n}(z)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
given by (
2.15
),

\widehat{e}_{jn}(z):=\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha_{j})}{\sqrt{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j})}}f_{\alpha}(z)^{n},\quad j=0,1,\quad n\in\mathbb{N}_{0},

(2.19)

where

\alpha_{0}:=\alpha,\quad\alpha_{1}:=\widehat{\alpha}.

Hence the Szegő kernel of
\mathbb{A}_{q}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\widehat{e}_{jn}(z)\overline{\widehat{e}_{jn}(w)}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha)\overline{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(w,\alpha)}}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,\alpha)}+\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\widehat{\alpha})\overline{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(w,\widehat{\alpha})}}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\alpha})}\right)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{\alpha}(z)^{n}\overline{f_{\alpha}(z)^{n}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha)\overline{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(w,\alpha)}}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,\alpha)}+\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\widehat{\alpha})\overline{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(w,\widehat{\alpha})}}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\alpha})}\right)\frac{1}{1-f_{\alpha}(z)\overline{f_{\alpha}(w)}},\,z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.20)

Then, if we consider the Hilbert subspace of

\mathbb{A}_{q}
such that

H^{2}_{\alpha,\widehat{\alpha}}(\mathbb{A}_{q}):=\{f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q}):f(\alpha)=f(\widehat{\alpha})=0\},

then its reproducing kernel should be given by

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha,\widehat{\alpha}}(z,w):=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)-\left(\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha)S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,w)}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,\alpha)}+\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\widehat{\alpha})S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\widehat{\alpha},w)}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\widehat{\alpha},\widehat{\alpha})}\right).

(2.21)

It is easy to verify that (
2.20
) gives

[
8
]

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha,\widehat{\alpha}}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)f_{\alpha}(z)\overline{f_{\alpha}(w)},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.22)

Now we ask the following question.

(Q)

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
, consider the
conditional Hilbert space

H^{2}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{A}_{q}):=\{f\in H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q}):f(\alpha)=0\},

whose reproducing kernel is given by

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha}(z,w):=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)-\frac{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha)S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,w)}{S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\alpha,\alpha)},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.23)

Is it possible to factorize this

conditional kernel
in the form
as (
2.14
) and (
2.22
)?

2.1.2
Theta function

Assume that
p\in\mathbb{C}
is a fixed number such that

0<|p|<1
.
We use the following standard notation,

\displaystyle(a;p)_{n}:=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-ap^{i}),\qquad(a;p)_{\infty}:=\prod_{i=0}^{\infty}(1-ap^{i}),

\displaystyle(a_{1},\dots,a_{k};p)_{\infty}:=(a_{1};p)_{\infty}\cdots(a_{k};p)_{\infty}.

(2.24)

The
theta function
with argument
z
and

nome

p
is defined by

\theta(z;p):=(z,p/z;p)_{\infty}.

(2.25)

We often use the shorthand notation

\theta(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};p):=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\theta(z_{i};p)
.

As a function of
z
, the theta function
\theta(z;p)
is
holomorphic in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and has single zeros
precisely at
p^{i}
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, that is,

\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}:\theta(z;p)=0\}=\{p^{i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

(2.26)

We will use the
inversion formula

\theta(1/z;p)=-\frac{1}{z}\theta(z;p)

(2.27)

and the
quasi-periodicity property

\theta(pz;p)=-\frac{1}{z}\theta(z;p)

(2.28)

of the theta function.
By comparing (
2.27
) and
(
2.28
) and performing
the transformation
z\mapsto 1/z
,
we immediately see the
periodicity property
,

\displaystyle\theta(p/z;p)=\theta(z;p).

(2.29)

By
Jacobi’s triple product identity

(see, for instance,
[
22
, Section 1.6]
),
we have
the Laurent expansion

\theta(z;p)=\frac{1}{(p;p)_{\infty}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}p^{\binom{n}{2}}z^{n}.

One can show that
[
59
, Chapter 20]

\displaystyle\lim_{p\to 0}\theta(z;p)=1-z,

(2.30)

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}(1;p):=\frac{\partial\theta(z;p)}{\partial z}\Big|_{z=1}=-(p;p)_{\infty}^{2}.

(2.31)

The theta function satisfies the following

Weierstrass’ addition formula

[
41
]
,

\theta(xy,x/y,uv,u/v;p)-\theta(xv,x/v,uy,u/y;p)=\frac{u}{y}\theta(yv,y/v,xu,x/u;p).

(2.32)

When
p
is real and
p\in(0,1)
, we see that

\overline{\theta(z;p)}=\theta(\overline{z};p).

(2.33)

In this case the definition (
2.25
)
with (
2.24
) implies that

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\theta(x;p)>0,&x\in(p^{2i+1},p^{2i})\cr\theta(x;p)=0,&x=p^{i}\cr\theta(x;p)<0,&x\in(p^{2i},p^{2i-1})\end{array}\right\}\quad i\in\mathbb{Z},

\displaystyle\quad\theta(x;p)>0,\quad x\in(-\infty,0).

(2.37)

Moreover, we can prove the following:
In the interval
x\in(-\infty,0)
,

\theta(x):=\theta(x;p)
is strictly convex with

\min_{x\in(-\infty,0)}\theta(x)=\theta(-\sqrt{p})=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1+p^{n-1/2})^{2}>0,

(2.38)

and

\lim_{x\downarrow-\infty}\theta(x)=\lim_{x\uparrow 0}\theta(x)=+\infty
,
and in
the interval
x\in(p,1)
,

\theta(x)
is strictly concave with

\max_{x\in(p,1)}\theta(x)=\theta(\sqrt{p})=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-p^{n-1/2})^{2},

(2.39)

\theta(x)\sim(p;p)_{\infty}^{2}(x-p)/p

as
x\downarrow p
, and

\theta(x)\sim(p;p)_{\infty}^{2}(1-x)

as
x\uparrow 1
,
where (
2.28
) and
(
2.31
) were used.

2.1.3
Weighted Szegő kernels of annulus
and conformal transformations

We introduce a positive parameter
r>0
.
Consider the Hilbert space of holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{A}_{q}

equipped with the inner product

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{2}_{r}(\mathbb{A}_{q})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\gamma_{1}\cup\gamma_{q}}f(z)\overline{g(z)}\sigma_{r}(dz),\quad f,g\in H^{2}_{r}(\mathbb{A}_{q})

with

\sigma_{r}(dz)=\begin{cases}d\phi,&\mbox{if $z\in\gamma_{1}:=\{e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi}:\phi\in[0,2\pi)\}$},\cr rd\phi,&\mbox{if $z\in\gamma_{q}:=\{qe^{\sqrt{-1}\phi}:\phi\in[0,2\pi)\}$},\end{cases}

which we write as
H_{r}^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
.
A CONS of
H_{r}^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})

is given by
\{e^{(q,r)}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with

e^{(q,r)}_{n}(z)=\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{1+rq^{2n}}},\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z},

and the reproducing kernel is given by
[
52
]

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{(q,r)}_{n}(z)\overline{e^{(q,r)}_{n}(w)}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(z\overline{w})^{n}}{1+rq^{2n}}.

(2.40)

This infinite series converges absolutely for
z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

When
r=q
, this Hilbert function space isreduced to
the Hardy space for
\mathbb{A}_{q}
,
H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
,
introduced in Example
2.3

in Section
2.1.1
, associated with the
reproducing kernel

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\cdot,\cdot):=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\cdot,\cdot;q).

The kernel (
2.40
)
with a parameter
r>0
is called the

weighted Szegő kernel
of
\mathbb{A}_{q}

and
H_{r}^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
is the

reproducing kernel Hilbert space

(RKHS) with respect to

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\cdot,\cdot;r)

[
52
]
.
We call
r
the
weight parameter

[
36
]
.
Notice that (
2.40
) implies that

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,z;r)
is a monotonically decreasing
function of the weight parameter

r\in(0,\infty)
for each fixed

z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

From now on, we put

p=q^{2}

(2.41)

for the nome
p
of the theta function (
2.25
),
and assume that

\theta(z)=\theta(z;q^{)}
,
where
q\in(0,1)
specifies the
radius of the inner circle of the annulus
\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

Proposition 2.4

(McCullough and Shen
[
52
]
)

For
r>0

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)=\frac{q_{0}^{2}\theta(-rz\overline{w})}{\theta(-r,z\overline{w})},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

(2.42)

where

q_{0}:=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(1-q^{2n})=(q^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}.

(2.43)

In particular,

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;q)=\frac{q_{0}^{2}\theta(-qz\overline{w})}{\theta(-q,z\overline{w})},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.44)

Proof.

The following function has been studied
in
[
52
,
14
,
84
]
,

f^{\rm JK}(z,a;q):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{z^{n}}{1-aq^{2n}},

(2.45)

with
q^{2}<|z|<1
,

a\notin\{q^{2i}:i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
,
which is called
the
Jordan–Kronecker function

[
84
, p.59]
.
We readily find that

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)=f^{\rm JK}(z\overline{w},-r;q),\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.46)

The
bilateral basic hypergeometric series

in base
p
with one numerator parameter
a

and one denominator parameter
b
is defined by

[
22
]

{{}_{1}}\psi_{1}(a;b;p,z)={{}_{1}}\psi_{1}\Big[\begin{array}[]{l}a\cr b\end{array};p,z\Big]:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(a;p)_{n}}{(b;p)_{n}}z^{n},\quad|b/a|<|z|<1.

The Jordan–Kronecker function (
2.45
)
is a special case
of the
{{}_{1}}\psi_{1}
function
[
14
,
84
]
;

f^{\rm JK}(z,a;q)=\frac{1}{1-a}{{}_{1}}\psi_{1}(a;aq^{2};q^{2},z).

The following equality is known as

Ramanujan’s
{{}_{1}}\psi_{1}
summation formula

[
14
,
22
,
84
]
,

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(a;p)_{n}}{(b;p)_{n}}z^{n}=\frac{(az,p/(az),p,b/a;p)_{\infty}}{(z,b/(az),b,p/a;p)_{\infty}},\quad|b/a|<|z|<1.

Combining the above two equalities
with an appropriate change of variables,
we obtain
[
14
,
84
]

f^{\rm JK}(z,a;q)=\frac{(az,q^{2}/(az),q^{2},q^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}}{(z,q^{2}/z,a,q^{2}/a;q^{2})_{\infty}}=\frac{q_{0}^{2}\theta(za;q^{2})}{\theta(z,a;q^{2})}.

(2.47)

By (
2.46
), (
2.47
) proves the
equality (
2.42
). The proof is complete.
∎

Here we notice that the following symmetries
of
f^{\rm JK}
are readily verified by (
2.47
)
using (
2.27
) and (
2.28
)

[
14
,
84
]
.

\displaystyle f^{\rm JK}(z,a)

\displaystyle=f^{\rm JK}(a,z),

(2.48)

\displaystyle f^{\rm JK}(z,a)

\displaystyle=-f^{\rm JK}(z^{-1},a^{-1}),

(2.49)

\displaystyle f^{\rm JK}(z,a)

\displaystyle=zf^{\rm JK}(z,aq^{2})=af^{\rm JK}(zq^{2},a).

(2.50)

Since
\theta
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
the expression (
2.42
) implies
that
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha;r)
is
meromorphic in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
By the basic properties of
\theta

given in Section
2.1.2
, the
following is easily verified.

Lemma 2.5

Assume that
\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
.
Then
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha;r)
has zeros at

z=-q^{2i}/(\overline{\alpha}r)
,
i\in\mathbb{Z}
in
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
.
In particular,

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,\alpha)
has a unique zero in
\mathbb{A}_{q}
at

z=\widehat{\alpha}
given by (
2.17
).

We have found the answer to the
question
(Q)
addressed in Section
2.1.1

in the paper by McCullough and Shen
[
52
]
.

Proposition 2.6

(Mccullough and Shen
[
52
]
)

The equality

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha}(z,w;r)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r|\alpha|^{2})h_{\alpha}^{q}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}^{q}(w)},\quad z,w,\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

(2.51)

holds with

h_{\alpha}^{q}(z):=z\frac{\theta(\alpha/z)}{\theta(\overline{\alpha}z)}=-\alpha\frac{\theta(z/\alpha)}{\theta(z\overline{\alpha})},\quad z,\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.52)

The special case with
r=q
answers
the question
(Q)
as

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;q|\alpha|^{2})h_{\alpha}^{q}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}^{q}(w)},\quad z,w,\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.53)

Proof.

We put (
2.23
) with (
2.42
)
and (
2.52
) to (
2.51
), then
the equality is expressed by
theta functions. After multiplying both sides by
the common denominator, we see that the
equality (
2.51
) is equivalent to the following,

\displaystyle\theta(-rz\overline{w},-r|\alpha|^{2},\overline{\alpha}z,\alpha\overline{w})-\theta(-r\overline{\alpha}z,-r\alpha\overline{w},z\overline{w},|\alpha|^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad=z\overline{w}\theta(-rz\overline{w}|\alpha|^{2},\alpha z^{-1},\overline{\alpha}\,\overline{w}^{-1},-r).

(2.54)

Now we change the variables from

\{z,\overline{w},\alpha,r\}
to

\{x,y,u,v\}
as

\overline{\alpha}z=x/y
,

\alpha\overline{w}=u/v
,

z\overline{w}=x/v
,

|\alpha|^{2}=u/y
, and

r=-yv
.
Then LHS
of (
2.54
) becomes

\theta(xy,x/y,uv,u/v)-\theta(xv,x/v,uy,u/y)
,
and RHS side becomes

(x/v)\theta(yv,(y/v)^{-1},xu,(x/u)^{-1})

which is equal to

(u/y)\theta(yv,y/v,xu,x/u)

by (
2.27
).
Hence Weierstrass’ addition formula
(
2.32
) proves the equality
(
2.54
).
The proof is complete.

Following the formula (
2.12
)
for
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha}
,
conditional kernels

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}
are inductively defined as

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(z,w)=(S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n-1}})^{\alpha_{n}}(z,w),\quad z,w,\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in D,\quad n=2,3,\dots.

(2.55)

The kernels
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}
,

n=2,3,\dots
,
will construct Hilbert subspaces of the Hardy space
for
H^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
,

H^{2}_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(\mathbb{A}_{q}):=\{f\in{\cal H}_{k}:f(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=f(\alpha_{n})=0\}.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
, define

\gamma^{q}_{\{\alpha_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}}(z):=\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{\alpha_{\ell}}^{q}(z),\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.56)

Then the Mccullough and Shen formula (
2.51
)

[
52
]
is generalized as

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}(z,w;r)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\Big(z,w;r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)\gamma^{q}_{\{\alpha_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}}(z)\overline{\gamma^{q}_{\{\alpha_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}}(w)},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

(2.57)

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
.
As a special case of (
2.57
), we have

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{\alpha,\widehat{\alpha}}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)f^{q}_{\alpha}(z)\overline{f^{q}_{\alpha}(w)},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}

(2.58)

with

\displaystyle f^{q}_{\alpha}(z)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{z}h^{q}_{\alpha}(z)h^{q}_{\widehat{\alpha}}(z)

\displaystyle=z\frac{\theta(-qz\overline{\alpha},\alpha/z)}{\theta(-qz/\alpha,\overline{\alpha}z)}=-\alpha\frac{\theta(-qz\overline{\alpha},z/\alpha)}{\theta(-qz/\alpha,z\overline{\alpha})}.

(2.59)

The equation (
2.58
) is nothing but
(
2.22
), and

f^{q}_{\alpha}
given by (
2.59
)
is identified with the

Ahlfors map
from
\mathbb{A}_{q}
to
\mathbb{D}
.

We can prove the following.

Lemma 2.7

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
,

(i)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}^{q}(\alpha)=0,

(2.60)

(ii)

\displaystyle 0<|h_{\alpha}^{q}(z)|<1\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}\setminus\{\alpha\},

(2.61)

(iii)

\displaystyle|h_{\alpha}^{q}(z)|=\begin{cases}1,&\quad\mbox{if $z\in\gamma_{1}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=1\}$},\cr|\alpha|,&\quad\mbox{if $z\in\gamma_{q}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:|z|=q\}$},\end{cases}

(2.62)

(iv)

\displaystyle{h_{\alpha}^{q}}^{\prime}(\alpha)=-\frac{\theta^{\prime}(1)}{\theta(|\alpha|^{2})}=\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{\theta(|\alpha|^{2})}>0,

(2.63)

(v)

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 0}h_{\alpha}^{q}(z)=\frac{z-\alpha}{1-\overline{\alpha}z}=z\frac{1-\alpha/z}{1-\overline{\alpha}z}=:h_{\alpha}(z).

(2.64)

Hence,

h_{\alpha}^{q}
is identified with a
conformal map

from
\mathbb{A}_{q}
to the unit disk with a
circular slit
in it,
in which
\alpha\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
is sent to the origin
[
52
]
.
Notice that
h_{\alpha}
is
the
Möbius transformation

\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
sending
\alpha
to the origin.

Figure 3:
Conformal map
h^{q}_{\alpha}:\mathbb{A}_{q}\to\mathbb{D}\setminus\{\mbox{a circular slit}\}

is illustrated for
q=1/3
and
\alpha=2/3
.
The point
\alpha=2/3
in
\mathbb{A}_{1/3}

is mapped to the origin.
The outer boundary
\gamma_{1}
of
\mathbb{A}_{1/3}

(denoted by a red circle) is mapped to a unit circle
(a red circle) making the boundary of
\mathbb{D}
.
The inner boundary
\gamma_{1/3}
of
\mathbb{A}_{1/3}

(a green circle) is mapped to a circular slit (denoted by a green arc)
which is a part of the circle with radius
\alpha=2/3
,
where the map is two-to-one
except the two points on
\gamma_{1/3}

mapped to the two edges of the circular slit.

2.2
I.i.d.Gaussian power series, Laurent series,
and their zero point processes

For a domain
D\subset\mathbb{C}
, let
X
be a random variable
on a probability space which takes values
in the space of analytic functions on
D
.
If
(X(z_{1}),\dots,X(z_{n}))
follows a centered (mean zero)
complex Gaussian distribution for every
n\in\mathbb{N}

and every
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in D
,

X
is said to be a
Gaussian analytic function
(GAF)

[
26
]
.
The zero set of
X
is regarded as
a point process on
D

denoted by a nonnegative-integer-valued
Radon measure

{\cal Z}_{X}=\sum_{z\in D:X(z)=0}\delta_{z},

and it is simply called the

zero point process
of the GAF.
Zero-point processes of GAFs have been extensively studied
in quantum and statistical physics as solvable models of
quantum chaotic systems and
interacting particle systems.
Many important characterizations of their probability laws
have been reported in probability theory.

A typical example of GAF
is provided by the

i.i.d. Gaussian power series

defined on the unit disk
\mathbb{D}
:
Let
\mathbb{N}_{0}:=\{0,1,2,\dots\}
and
\{\zeta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}

be i.i.d. standard complex Gaussian random variables
with density

{\rm p}(z)=\frac{1}{\pi}e^{-|z|^{2}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-x^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-y^{2}},\quad z=x+\sqrt{-1}y,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},

and consider a random power series,

X_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\zeta_{n}z^{n},

(2.65)

which defines an analytic function on
\mathbb{D}
a.s.
This gives a GAF on
\mathbb{D}
with a
covariance kernel

{\bf E}[X_{\mathbb{D}}(z)\overline{X_{\mathbb{D}}(w)}]=\frac{1}{1-z\overline{w}}=:S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w),\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

(2.66)

This kernel is identified with
the
Szegő kernel
of
\mathbb{D}
(
2.6
)
of the Hardy space
H^{2}(\mathbb{D})
introduced
in Section
2.1.1
).
Peres and Virág
[
63
]
proved that

{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}
is a
determinantal point process
(DPP)
such that the
correlation kernel

is given by

S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)^{2}=(1-z\overline{w})^{-2}
,

z,w\in\mathbb{D}

with respect to the reference measure

\lambda=m/\pi
.
Here
m
represents the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{C}
;

m(dz):=dxdy
,
z=x+\sqrt{-1}y\in\mathbb{C}
.
This correlation kernel is identified with
the reproducing kernel of the Bergman space on
\mathbb{D}

(
2.4
), which is
called
Bergman kernel
of
\mathbb{D}
in Section
2.1.1
.
Thus the study of Peres and Virág on

X_{\mathbb{D}}
and
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}

is associated with the following relationship
between kernels on
\mathbb{D}

[
63
]
,

{\bf E}[X_{\mathbb{D}}(z)\overline{X_{\mathbb{D}}(w)}]^{2}=S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)^{2}=K_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w),\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

(2.67)

Associated with the RKHS
H_{r}^{2}(\mathbb{A}_{q})

studied in Section
2.1.3

we consider the

Gaussian Laurent series

X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(z):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\zeta_{n}e^{(q,r)}_{n}(z)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\zeta_{n}\frac{z^{n}}{\sqrt{1+rq^{2n}}},

(2.68)

where
\{\zeta_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
are i.i.d. standard complex
Gaussian random variables with density
e^{-|z|^{2}}/\pi
.
Since
\lim_{n\to\infty}|\zeta_{n}|^{1/n}=1
a.s.,
we apply the Cauchy–Hadamard
criterion to the positive
and negative powers of
X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(z)
separately
to conclude that this random Laurent series converges
a.s. whenever
z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
.
Moreover, since the distribution
\zeta_{n}
is symmetric,
both of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{q}
are natural
boundaries.
Hence
X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}
provides a GAF on
\mathbb{A}_{q}
whose
covariance kernel is given by
the
weighted Szegő kernel of
\mathbb{A}_{q}
,

{\bf E}[X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(z)\overline{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(w)}]=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r),\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

and the zero point process is denoted by

{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}:=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}:X^{r}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z)=0}\delta_{z}
.
In particular, we write

X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z):=X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{q}(z),z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
and

{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}}:={\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{q}}

as mentioned above.

We recall
Schottky’s theorem
:
The group of conformal transformations
from
\mathbb{A}_{q}
to itself
is generated by the rotations
and the
q
-inversions

T_{q}(z):=\frac{q}{z}.

The invariance of the present
GAF and its zero point process under rotation is obvious.
Using the properties of
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}
,
we can prove the following.

Proposition 2.8

(i)
The GAF
X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}
given by (
2.68
)
has the

(q,r)
-inversion symmetry
in the sense that

\Big\{(T_{q}^{\prime}(z))^{1/2}X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(T_{q}(z))\Big\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\Big\{\sqrt{\frac{q}{r}}X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{q^{2}/r}(z)\Big\},\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

where
T_{q}^{\prime}(z):=\frac{dT_{q}}{dz}(z)=-q/z^{2}
.

(ii)
For
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}=\sum_{i}\delta_{Z_{i}}
,
let

T_{q}^{*}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}:=\sum_{i}\delta_{T_{q}^{-1}(Z_{i})}
.
Then

T_{q}^{*}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{q^{2}/r}}
.

(iii)
In particular, when
r=q
,
the GAF
X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}
is invariant under
conformal transformations which preserve
\mathbb{A}_{q}
,
and so is its zero point process
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}}
.

Proof.

The equivalence in distribution asserted by (i)
is implied by proving the following equality
of the covariance kernel given by the
weighted Szegő kernel of
\mathbb{A}_{q}
,

\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(T_{q}(z),T_{q}(w);r)=\frac{q}{r}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;q^{2}/r).

(2.69)

If we use the expression (
2.46
)
of
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)
,
the symmetries
of the Jordan–Kronecker function
(
2.49
) and (
2.50
)
prove (
2.69
).
By the definition of zero point processes,
(ii) is concluded from (i).
When
r=q
, (
2.69
) becomes

\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(T_{q}(z),T_{q}(w);q)=\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(T_{q}(z),T_{q}(w))=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w),

which implies the invariance of the GAF
X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}
under conformal
transformations preserving
\mathbb{A}_{q}

by Schottkey’s theorem and (iii) is proved. ∎

We can give

probabilistic interpretations

of the above facts as follows.

Proposition 2.9

For any
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the following hold.

(i)
The following equality is established,

\{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(z):z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}\}

given
\{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(\alpha_{n})=0\}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\left\{\gamma^{q}_{\{\alpha_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}}(z)X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}}(z):z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}\right\}.

(ii)
Let
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}

denote the zero point process
of the GAF
X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(z)

given
\{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(\alpha_{1})=\cdots=X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}(\alpha_{n})=0\}
.
Then,

{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}^{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\alpha_{i}}.

Remark 2.10

For the GAF on
\mathbb{D}

studied by Peres and Virág
[
63
]
,

\{X_{\mathbb{D}}(z):z\in\mathbb{D}\}

given
\{X_{\mathbb{D}}(\alpha)=0\}

is equal in law to
\{h_{\alpha}(z)X_{\mathbb{D}}(z):z\in\mathbb{D}\}
,

\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{D}
,
where
h_{\alpha}
is given by (
2.64
),
and then, in the notation
used in Proposition
2.9
,

{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}^{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}+\delta_{\alpha},\quad\forall\alpha\in\mathbb{D}.

Hence, no new GAF nor new zero point process
appear by conditioning of zeros.
For the present GAF on
\mathbb{A}_{q}
, however,
conditioning of zeros induces new GAFs and
new zero point processes
as shown by Proposition
2.9
.
Actually, by (
2.40
)
the covariance of the induced GAF

X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}}

is expressed by

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\Big(z,w;r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(z\overline{w})^{n}}{1+r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|\alpha_{\ell}|^{2}q^{2n}}.

Since
q<|\alpha_{\ell}|<1
,
as increasing the number of conditioning zeros,
the variance of induced GAF monotonically
increases, in which the increment is a decreasing function of

|\alpha_{\ell}|\in(q,1)
.

2.3
Permanental-determinantal point processes

2.3.1
Correlation functions of point processes

A
point process
is formulated as follows
[
35
]
.
Let
S
be a
base space
,
which is locally compact Hausdorff space
with a countable base, and
a
reference measure

\lambda

is given on
S
.
The
configuration space
of a point process on
S
is given by

{\rm Conf}(S)=\Big\{\xi=\sum_{i}\delta_{x_{i}}:\mbox{$x_{i}\in S$,
$\xi(\Lambda)<\infty$ for all bounded set $\Lambda\subset S$}\Big\}.

We say
\xi_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}:=\{1,2,\dots\}
converges
to
\xi
in the
vague topology
, if

\int_{S}f(x)\xi_{n}(dx)\to\int_{S}f(x)\xi(dx),\quad\forall f\in{\cal C}_{\rm c}(S),

where
{\cal C}_{\rm c}(S)
is the set of
all continuous real-valued functions
with compact support.
A point process on
S
is
a
{\rm Conf}(S)
-valued random variable
\Xi=\Xi(\cdot)
.
If
\Xi(\{x\})\in\{0,1\}
for any point
z\in S
,
then the point process is said to be
simple
.
Assume that
\Lambda_{i},i=1,\dots,m
,

m\in\mathbb{N}
are
disjoint bounded sets in
S
and

k_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0},i=1,\dots,m
satisfy

\sum_{i=1}^{m}k_{i}=n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
A symmetric measure
\lambda^{n}
on
S^{n}

is called the
n
-th
correlation measure
,
if it satisfies

{\bf E}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\Xi(\Lambda_{i})!}{(\Xi(\Lambda_{i})-k_{i})!}\Bigg]=\lambda^{n}(\Lambda_{1}^{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Lambda_{m}^{k_{m}}),

where when
\Xi(\Lambda_{i})-k_{i}<0
,
we interpret
\Xi(\Lambda_{i})!/(\Xi(\Lambda_{i})-k_{i})!=0
.
If
\lambda^{n}
is absolutely continuous
with respect to the
n
-product measure
\lambda^{\otimes n}
,
the Radon–Nikodym derivative

\rho^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
is called
the
n
-point correlation function

with respect to the reference measure
\lambda
;
that is,

\lambda^{n}(dx_{1}\cdots dx_{n})=\rho^{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\lambda^{\otimes n}(dx_{1}\cdots dx_{n}).

2.3.2
Permanental-determinantal point processes
(PDPPs) with hierarchical structures

We introduce the following
notation. For an
n\times n
matrix

M=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
,

\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}M=\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[m_{ij}]:=\mathop{\mathrm{per}}M\det M,

(2.70)

that is,
\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}M
denotes

\mathop{\mathrm{per}}M
multiplied by
\det M
.
Note that

\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}
is a special case
of
hyperdeterminants
introduced
by Gegenbauer following Cayley
[
36
]
.
If
M
is a positive semidefinite Hermitian matrix, then

\mathop{\mathrm{per}}M\geq\det M\geq 0
,
and hence
\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}M\geq 0
by the definition (
2.70
).
The following is the main theorem in Section
2
.

Theorem 2.11

The zero point process
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{r}}

on
\mathbb{A}_{q}
is a

permanental-determinantal point process

(PDPP) in the sense that
it has correlation functions

\{\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

given by

\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};r)=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r\prod_{k=1}^{n}|z_{k}|^{4})}\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\Big(z_{i},z_{j};r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)\Big]

(2.71)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}

with respect to
m/\pi
.

The
density of zeros on
\mathbb{A}_{q}

with respect to
m/\pi
is given by

\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z;r)=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r|z|^{4})}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,z;r|z|^{2})^{2}=\frac{q_{0}^{4}\theta(-r,-r|z|^{4})}{\theta(-r|z|^{2},|z|^{2})^{2}},\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

(2.72)

which is always positive.
Since
\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z;r)
depends only on
the modulus of the coordinate
|z|\in(q,1)
,
the PDPP is rotationally invariant.
As shown by
(
2.37
)–(
2.39
)
in Section
2.1.2
,
in the interval
x\in(-\infty,0)
,
\theta(x)
is positive and
strictly convex with
\lim_{x\downarrow-\infty}\theta(x)=\lim_{x\uparrow 0}\theta(x)=+\infty
, while
in the interval
x\in(q^{2},1)
,
\theta(x)
is positive
and strictly concave
with

\theta(x)\sim q_{0}^{2}(x-q^{2})/q^{2}
as
x\downarrow q^{2}

and
\theta(x)\sim q_{0}^{2}(1-x)
as
x\uparrow 1
.
Therefore, the density shows divergence both at the inner
and outer boundaries as

\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z;r)\sim\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\frac{q^{2}}{(|z|^{2}-q^{2})^{2}}},&|z|\downarrow q,\cr\displaystyle{\frac{1}{(1-|z|^{2})^{2}}},&|z|\uparrow 1,\end{cases}

(2.73)

which is independent of
r
and implies
{\bf E}[{\cal Z}_{X^{r}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}}(\mathbb{A}_{q})]=\infty
. See Fig.
4
.

Figure 4:
Dependence on the radial coordinate
r=|z|\in(q,1)

of the density function
\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z;r)

in the case
q=r=1/3
.

If
M
is a
2\times 2
matrix, we see that

\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}M=\det(M\circ M)
,
where
M\circ M
denotes the Hadamard product of
M
,
i.e., entrywise multiplication,
(M\circ M)_{ij}=M_{ij}M_{ij}
.
Then the two-point correlation is expressed by a single
determinant as

\rho^{2}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{1},z_{2};r)=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r|z_{1}|^{4}|z_{2}|^{4})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}\left[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{i},z_{j};r|z_{1}|^{2}|z_{2}|^{2})^{2}\right],\quad z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.74)

Remark 2.12

(i) The PDPP with correlation functions (
2.71
)
turns out to be a
simple point process
,
i.e., there is no multiple point a.s.,
due to the existence of two-point correlation
function with respect to
the Lebesgue measure
m/\pi

[
28
, Lemma 2.7]
.
(ii) Using the explicit expression (
2.71
) together
with the
Frobenius determinantal formula

(
2.93
) given below,
we can verify that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
the
n
-point correlation
\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})>0

if all coordinates
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
are
different from each other,
and that
\rho_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=0
if
some of
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
coincide;
e.g.,
z_{i}=z_{j},i\not=j
,
by the determinantal factor in
\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}
(
2.70
).

Remark 2.13

The
determinantal point processes
(DPPs)
and the
permanental point processes
(PPPs)
have the
n
-correlation functions of the forms

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{DPP}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[K(z_{i},z_{j})],\quad\rho_{\mathrm{PPP}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[K(z_{i},z_{j})],

respectively (cf.
[
77
,
26
]
).
Due to
Hadamard’s inequality
for the determinant
[
51
, Section II.4]

and
Lieb’s inequality for the permanent

[
49
]
,
we have

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathrm{DPP}}^{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\leq\rho_{\mathrm{DPP}}^{1}(z_{1})\rho_{\mathrm{DPP}}^{1}(z_{2}),\quad\rho_{\mathrm{PPP}}^{2}(z_{1},z_{2})\geq\rho_{\mathrm{PPP}}^{1}(z_{1})\rho_{\mathrm{PPP}}^{1}(z_{2}).

These correlation inequalities suggest a repulsive nature
(negative correlation) for DPPs and
an attractive nature (positive correlation) for PPPs.
Since
\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}
is considered to have intermediate nature
between determinant and permanent,
PDPPs are expected to exhibit

both repulsive and attractive characters
,
depending on the position of two points
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
.
For example, Remark
2.12
(ii) shows
the repulsive nature inherited from the DPP side.
The two-sidedness of the present PDPP will be summarized in Theorem
2.22
given below.

Remark 2.14

The
(q,r)
-inversionb symmetry
given by
Proposition
2.8
(ii) is
rephrased using correlation functions as

\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(T_{q}(z_{1}),\dots,T_{q}(z_{n});r)\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|T_{q}^{\prime}(z_{\ell})|^{2}=\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};q^{2}/r)

(2.75)

for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
,
where
T_{q}(z)=q/z
and
|T_{q}^{\prime}(z)|^{2}=q^{2}/|z|^{4}
.
In the correlation functions

\{\rho_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
given by
Theorem
2.11
, we see
an
inductive structure
such that
the functional form of the
permanental-determinantal correlation kernel

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(\cdot,\cdot;r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2})

is depending on the
points
\{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\}
, which we intend to measure
by
\rho_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{n}
, via the weight parameter

r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}
.
This is due to the inductive structure
of the induced GAFs generated in conditioning of zeros as
mentioned in Remark
2.10
.
In addition, the reference measure
m/\pi
is
also weighted by

\theta(-r)/\theta(-r\prod_{k=1}^{n}|z_{k}|^{4})
.
Such a
hierarchical structure

of correlation functions and reference measures
is necessary to realize
the
(q,r)
-inversion symmetry (
2.75
)
and the invariance under
conformal transformations

preserving
\mathbb{A}_{q}
when
r=q
.

2.3.3
Simpler but still non-trivial PDPP in

q\to 0
limit

The above GAF and the PDPP induce the following limiting cases.
With fixed
r>0
we take the limit
q\to 0
.
By the reason explained in Remark
2.16
below,
in this limiting procedure, we should consider the
point processes
\{{\cal Z}_{X^{r}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}}:q>0\}
to be defined on
the
punctured unit disk

\mathbb{D}^{\times}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:0<|z|<1\}

instead of
\mathbb{D}
.
Although the limit point process is given on
\mathbb{D}^{\times}

by definition,
it can be naturally viewed as a point process
defined on
\mathbb{D}
, which we will introduce below.
Let
H_{r}^{2}(\mathbb{D})
be the

Hardy space on
\mathbb{D}
with the
weight parameter
r>0
,
whose inner product is given by

\langle f,g\rangle_{H^{2}_{r}(\mathbb{D})}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}f(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})\overline{g(e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi})}d\phi+rf(0)g(0),\quad f,g\in H^{2}_{r}(\mathbb{D}).

The reproducing kernel of
H^{2}_{r}(\mathbb{D})
is given by

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w;r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}e^{(0,r)}_{n}(z)\overline{e^{(0,r)}_{n}(w)}=\frac{1}{1+r}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(z\overline{w})^{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1+rz\overline{w}}{(1+r)(1-z\overline{w})},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

(2.76)

The GAF associated with
H_{r}^{2}(\mathbb{D})
is then
defined by

X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}(z)=\frac{\zeta_{0}}{\sqrt{1+r}}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{n}z^{n},\quad z\in\mathbb{D}

(2.77)

so that the covariance kernel is given by

{\bf E}[X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}(z)\overline{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}(w)}]=S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w;r)
,

z,w\in\mathbb{D}
.
For the conditional GAF given a zero at
\alpha\in\mathbb{D}
,
the covariance kernel is given by

S_{\mathbb{D}}^{\alpha}(z,w;r)=S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w;r|\alpha|^{2})h_{\alpha}(z)\overline{h_{\alpha}(w)},\quad z,w,\alpha\in\mathbb{D},

where the replacement of the weight parameter

r
by
r|\alpha|^{2}
should be done, even though
the factor
h_{\alpha}(z)
is simply given by
the Möbius transformation (
2.64
).

For the zero point process
Theorem
2.11
is reduced to
the following by the formula

\lim_{q\to 0}\theta(z;q^{2})=1-z.

Corollary 2.15

Assume that
r>0
.
Then
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}}
is
a PDPP on
\mathbb{D}
with the correlation functions

\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{D}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};r)=\frac{1+r}{1+r\prod_{k=1}^{n}|z_{k}|^{4}}\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[S_{\mathbb{D}}\Big(z_{i},z_{j};r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)\Big]

(2.78)

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}

with respect to
m/\pi
.
In particular, the density of zeros on
\mathbb{D}

is given by

\rho_{\mathbb{D}}^{1}(z;r)=\frac{(1+r)(1+r|z|^{4})}{(1+r|z|^{2})^{2}(1-|z|^{2})^{2}},\quad z\in\mathbb{D}.

(2.79)

As
r
increases the first term in (
2.77
),
which gives the value of the GAF at the origin,
decreases and hence the variance at the origin,

{\bf E}[|X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}(0)|^{2}]=S_{\mathbb{D}}(0,0;r)=(1+r)^{-1}
decreases monotonically.
As a result the density of zeros
in the vicinity of the origin
increases as
r
increases.
Actually we see that
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}^{1}(0;r)=1+r
.

Remark 2.16

The asymptotics (
2.73
) show that
the density of zeros of

{\cal Z}_{X^{r}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}}
diverges at the inner boundary

\gamma_{q}=\{z:|z|=q\}
for each
q>0

while the density of
{\cal Z}_{X^{r}_{\mathbb{D}}}
is finite at the origin
as in (
2.79
). Therefore infinitely many zeros near
the inner boundary
\gamma_{q}

seem to vanish in the limit as
q\to 0
.
This is the reason why we regard the base space of

\{{\cal Z}_{X^{r}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}}:q>0\}
and the limit point process

{\cal Z}_{X^{r}_{\mathbb{D}}}
as
\mathbb{D}^{\times}
instead of
\mathbb{D}

as mentioned before.
Indeed, in the vague topology,
with which we equip a configuration space,
we cannot see configurations outside each compact set, hence
infinitely many zeros are not observed
on each compact set in
\mathbb{D}^{\times}
(not
\mathbb{D}
)
for any sufficiently small
q>0

depending on the compact set that we take.

2.3.4
Reduction to GAF and DPP of
Peres and Virág

If we take the further limit
r\to 0
in
(
2.76
), we obtain the Szegő kernel of
\mathbb{D}

given by (
2.66
).
Since the matrix

(S_{\mathbb{D}}(z_{i},z_{j})^{-1})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=(1-z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}

has rank 2, the following equality called

Borchardt’s identity
holds
(see Theorem 3.2 in
[
58
]
, Theorem 5.1.5 in
[
26
]
),

\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[(1-z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})^{-1}\Big]=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[(1-z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})^{-2}\Big].

(2.80)

By the relation (
2.67
), the
r\to 0
limit
of
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}}
is identified with
the DPP on
\mathbb{D}
,
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}
,
studied by Peres and Virág
[
63
]
,
whose correlation functions are given by

\rho_{\mathbb{D},\mathrm{PV}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[K_{\mathbb{D}}(z_{i},z_{j})],\quad n\in\mathbb{N},\quad z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{D},

with respect to
m/\pi

Here we give the result by Peres and Virág as
a theorem.

Theorem 2.17

(Peres and Virág
[
63
]
)

{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}
is a DPP on
\mathbb{D}

such that the correlation kernel
with respect to
m/\pi

is given by
the Bergman kernel
K_{\mathbb{D}}
of
\mathbb{D}
given by
(
2.4
).

Remark 2.18

We see from (
2.76
) that

\lim_{r\to\infty}S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w;r)=(1-z\overline{w})^{-1}-1,\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D},

which can be identified with
the conditional kernel
given a zero at the origin;

S_{\mathbb{D}}^{0}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)-S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,0)S_{\mathbb{D}}(0,w)/S_{\mathbb{D}}(0,0)

for
S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,0)\equiv 1
.
In this limit we can use Borchardt’s identity again, since
the rank of the matrix

(S_{\mathbb{D}}(z_{i},z_{j};\infty)^{-1})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}=(z_{i}^{-1}z_{j}^{-1}-1)_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
is two.
Then, thanks to the proper limit of the prefactor
of
\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}
in (
2.78
)
when
z_{k}\in\mathbb{D}^{\times}
for all
k=1,2,\dots,n
;

\lim_{r\to\infty}\frac{1+r}{1+r\prod_{k=1}^{n}|z_{k}|^{4}}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}|z_{k}|^{-4},

we can verify that

\lim_{r\to\infty}\rho_{\mathbb{D}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};r)=\rho_{\mathbb{D},\mathrm{PV}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})

for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and every

z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{D}^{\times}
.
On the other hand, taking (
2.77
) into account, we have

X^{\infty}_{\mathbb{D}}(z)=z\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{n}z^{n-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}zX_{\mathbb{D}}(z),

from which,
we can see that

\lim_{r\to\infty}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}}=:{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{\infty}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}+\delta_{0},

that is,
the DPP of Peres and Virág with
a deterministic zero added at the origin.
This is consistent with the fact that
\rho_{\mathbb{D}}^{1}(0;r)=1+r
diverges as
r\to\infty
.
Since

\lim_{r\to 0}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}}=:{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{0}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}

as mentioned above,
the one-parameter family of
PDPPs
\{{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}^{r}}:r\in(0,\infty)\}

can be regarded as an

interpolation
between
the DPP of Peres and Virág and
that DPP with a deterministic zero added at the origin.

The distribution of
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}
is invariant under
Möbius transformations that preserve
\mathbb{D}

[
63
]
.
This invariance is a special case of the following,
which can be proved using
the conformal transformations
of the Szegő kernel and the Bergman kernel
given by (
2.7
)

[
63
,
26
]
.

Proposition 2.19

(Peres and Virág
[
63
]
)

Let
\widetilde{D}\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
be a
simply connected domain wit
analytic boundary.
Then there is a GAF
X_{\widetilde{D}}
with
covariance kernel

{\bf E}[X_{\widetilde{D}}(z)\overline{X_{\widetilde{D}}(w)}]=S_{\widetilde{D}}(z,w)
,
z,w\in\widetilde{D}
,
where
S_{\widetilde{D}}
denotes
the Szegő kernel of
\widetilde{D}
.
The zero point process
{\cal Z}_{X_{\widetilde{D}}}

is the DPP such
that the correlation kernel is given by
the Bergman kernel
K_{\widetilde{D}}
of
\widetilde{D}
.
This DPP is conformally invariant in the following sense.
If
D\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
is another
simply connected domain with analytic
boundary, and

f:D\to\widetilde{D}

is a
conformal transformation
, then
for
{\cal Z}_{X_{D}}=\sum_{i}\delta_{Z_{i}}
,

\sum_{i}\delta_{f^{-1}(Z_{i})}=:f^{*}{\cal Z}_{X_{\widetilde{D}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\cal Z}_{X_{D}}.

That is,
f^{*}{\cal Z}_{X_{\widetilde{D}}}

is a DPP such that the correlation kernel
is equal to the Bergman kernel
K_{D}
of
D
.

We can say that the zero point process
of Peres and Virág
[
63
]

is a
conformal invariant DPP
.

2.4
Proof of Theorem
2.11

2.4.1
Edelman-Kostlan formulas and
second log-derivatives of Szegő kernels

For the DPP of
Peres and Virág the
Edelman–Kostlan formula

[
20
]
gives the density of
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}

with respect to
m/\pi
as

\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{D},\mathrm{PV}}(z)=\frac{1}{4}\Delta\log S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,z),\quad z\in\mathbb{D},

where
\Delta:=4\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{z}}
.
Moreover, we have the equality

K_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)=\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{w}}\log S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w)^{2},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{D}.

(2.81)

As explained above (
2.11
),
this gives an example of the general formula

S_{D}(z,w)^{2}=K_{D}(z,w),\quad z,w\in D,

(2.82)

which is establised
for the kernels on any simply connected domain

D\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
.

Here we have reported our work to
generalize the above to
a family of GAFs and their zero point processes
on the annulus
\mathbb{A}_{q}
.
We can prove the following
[
36
]
.

Proposition 2.20

For
r>0
,
the following equality holds,

\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{w}}\log S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r(z\overline{w})^{2})}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;rz\overline{w})^{2},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.83)

In particular,

\Delta\log S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,z;r)=4\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r|z|^{4})}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,z;r|z|^{2})^{2},\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.84)

By comparing the expression (
2.72
)
for the density obtained from Theorem
2.11

with (
2.84
)
in Proposition
2.20
,
we can recover the Edelman–Kostlan formula as follows,

\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z;r)=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r|z|^{4})}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,z;r|z|^{2})^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\Delta\log S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,z;r),\quad z\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

However, (
2.81
) does not
hold for the weighted Szegő kernel for
H^{2}_{r}(\mathbb{A}_{q})
.
As shown by (
2.83
),
the second log-derivative of
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)

cannot be expressed by
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)
itself
but a new function
S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;rz\overline{w})

should be introduced.

In addition the proportionality between the square of
the Szegő kernel
and the Bergman kernel (
2.82
),
which holds as (
2.82
) on
\mathbb{D}
,
is no longer valid for the point processes on
\mathbb{A}_{q}

The following are verified
[
36
]
.
A CONS for the
Bergman space on
\mathbb{A}_{q}

is given by

\{\widetilde{e}^{(q)}_{n}(z)\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
where we set

\widetilde{e}^{(q)}_{n}(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle{\sqrt{\frac{n+1}{1-q^{2(n+1)}}}z^{n},}&n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{-1\},\cr\displaystyle{\sqrt{\frac{1}{-2\log q}}z^{-1}},&n=-1.\end{cases}

The Bergman kernel of
\mathbb{A}_{q}
is then given by

\displaystyle K_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)

\displaystyle:=k_{L^{2}_{{\rm B}}(\mathbb{A}_{q})}(z,w)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{e}^{(q)}_{n}(z)\overline{\widetilde{e}^{(q)}_{n}(w)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2\log q}\frac{1}{z\overline{w}}+\frac{1}{z\overline{w}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{n}{1-q^{2n}}(z\overline{w})^{n},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q}.

(2.85)

The
Weierstrass
\wp
-function

is defined by

\displaystyle\wp(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{12}+2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}}-\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi}q^{2n}}{(1-e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi}q^{2n})^{2}}.

(2.86)

Then (
2.85
) is expressed as as
[
9
]

K_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)=-\frac{1}{2\log q}\frac{1}{z\overline{w}}-\frac{1}{z\overline{w}}\Big(\wp(\phi_{z\overline{w}})+\frac{P(q)}{12}\Big),\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

(2.87)

where we have used the notation,

z=e^{\sqrt{-1}\phi_{z}}\iff\phi_{z}=-\sqrt{-1}\log z,

(2.88)

and

P(q)=1-24\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}}.

We can prove the equality
[
36
]

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)^{2}=K_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)+\frac{a(q)}{z\overline{w}},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q},

(2.89)

where

a(q)=-2\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{(-1)^{n}nq^{n}}{1-q^{2n}}+\frac{1}{2\log q}.

(2.90)

2.4.2
Hammersley formula and Shirai’s Proposition

We recall a general formula for correlation functions
of zero point process of a GAF, which
is called the

Hammersley formula
in
[
63
]
, but here we use
a slightly different expression given
by Shirai (Proposition 6.1 of
[
75
]
).
Let
\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{w}}:=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z\partial\overline{w}}
.

Proposition 2.21

(Shirai
[
75
]
)

The correlation functions of
{\cal Z}_{X_{D}}
of the GAF
X_{D}
on

D\subsetneq\mathbb{C}
with
covariance kernel
S_{D}(z,w)
are given by

\rho_{D}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\big[(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{w}}S_{D}^{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}})(z_{i},z_{j})\big]}{\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[S_{D}(z_{i},z_{j})\Big]},\quad n\in\mathbb{N},\quad z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in D,

with respect to a reference measure
\lambda
,
whenever
\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[S_{D}(z_{i},z_{j})]>0
.
Here
\{S_{D}^{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}}(z_{i},z_{j})\}
denote
the conditional kernels.

Here we abbreviate

\gamma^{q}_{\{z_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{n}}
given by (
2.56
)
to
\gamma^{q}_{n}
.
Then (
2.57
) gives

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}}(z,w;r)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2})\gamma^{q}_{n}(z)\overline{\gamma^{q}_{n}(w)}

for
z,w,z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{A}_{q}
.
By (
2.60
) of Lemma
2.7
,
this formula gives

(\partial_{z}\partial_{\overline{w}}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{z_{1},\dots,z_{n}})(z_{i},z_{j};r)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\Big(z_{i},z_{j};r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}\Big){\gamma^{q}_{n}}^{\prime}(z_{i})\overline{{\gamma^{q}_{n}}^{\prime}(z_{j})}.

Therefore,
Proposition
2.21
gives now

\rho^{n}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n};r)=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{per}}_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\left(z_{i},z_{j};r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}\right)\right]\prod_{k=1}^{n}|{\gamma^{q}_{n}}^{\prime}(z_{k})|^{2}}{\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{i},z_{j};r)\Big]}.

(2.91)

By (
2.52
) and
(
2.60
) and (
2.63
)
of Lemma
2.7
,
we see that

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}|{\gamma_{n}^{q}}^{\prime}(z_{i})|^{2}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big|\Big(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n,j\not=i}h^{q}_{z_{j}}(z_{i})\Big){h^{q}_{z_{i}}}^{\prime}(z_{i})\Big|^{2}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\Big|\Big(\prod_{1\leq j\leq n,j\not=i}z_{i}\frac{\theta(z_{j}/z_{i})}{\theta(\overline{z_{j}}z_{i})}\Big)\frac{q_{0}^{2}}{\theta(|z_{i}|^{2})}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\Bigg|\frac{q_{0}^{2n}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}z_{i}\theta(z_{j}/z_{i})\cdot\prod_{1\leq i^{\prime}<j^{\prime}\leq n}z_{j^{\prime}}\theta(z_{i^{\prime}}/z_{j^{\prime}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\theta(z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})}\Bigg|^{2}.

By (
2.27
),

z_{i}\theta(z_{j}/z_{i})=z_{i}(-z_{j}/z_{i})\theta(z_{i}/z_{j})=-z_{j}\theta(z_{i}/z_{j})
.
Hence this is written as

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}|{\gamma_{n}^{q}}^{\prime}(z_{i})|^{2}

\displaystyle=q_{0}^{4n}\left|\frac{(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\big(\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}z_{j}\theta(z_{i}/z_{j})\big)^{2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\theta(z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=q_{0}^{4n}\Bigg(\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|z_{j}|^{2}\theta(z_{i}/z_{j},\overline{z_{i}}/\overline{z_{j}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\theta(z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})}\Bigg)^{2}.

(2.92)

The following identity is known as
the
Frobenius determinantal formula
,
which is
an
elliptic extension
of the

Cauchy determinantal formula

due Frobenius (see
Theorem 1.1 in
[
27
]
,
Theorem 66 in
[
45
]
,
Corollary 4.7 in
[
67
]
),

\displaystyle\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left[\frac{\theta(tx_{i}a_{j})}{\theta(t,x_{i}a_{j})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(t\prod_{k=1}^{n}x_{k}a_{k})}{\theta(t)}\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}x_{j}a_{j}\theta(x_{i}/x_{j},a_{i}/a_{j})}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\theta(x_{i}a_{j})}.

By (
2.42
) in Proposition
2.4
,
we have

q_{0}^{2n}\frac{\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}|z_{j}|^{2}\theta(z_{i}/z_{j},\overline{z_{i}}/\overline{z_{j}})}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\theta(z_{i}\overline{z_{j}})}=\frac{\theta(-s)}{\theta(-s\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\left[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{i},z_{j};s)\right],\quad\forall s>0.

(2.93)

Then (
2.92
) is written as

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}|{\gamma_{n}^{q}}^{\prime}(z_{i})|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{i},z_{j};r)]

\displaystyle\quad\times\frac{\theta(-r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2})}{\theta(-r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{4})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\Big(z_{i},z_{j};r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\frac{\theta(-r)}{\theta(-r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{4})}\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z_{i},z_{j};r)]\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Big[S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}\Big(z_{i},z_{j};r\prod_{\ell=1}^{n}|z_{\ell}|^{2}\Big)\Big].

Applying the above to (
2.91
),
the correlation functions
in Theorem
2.11
are obtained.

2.5
Unfolded 2-correlation function of
PDPP

By the determinantal factor in

\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}
(
2.70
)
the PDPP shall be negatively correlated
when distances of points
are short in the domain
\mathbb{A}_{q}
.
The effect of the permanental part
in
\mathop{\mathrm{perdet}}
will appear in long distances.
In order to clarify this fact,
we study the two-point correlation
function normalized by the product of
one-point functions,

\displaystyle g_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r):=\frac{\rho_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{2}(z,w;r)}{\rho_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{1}(z;r)\rho_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}^{1}(w;r)},\quad(z,w)\in\mathbb{A}_{q}^{2},

(2.94)

where
\rho^{1}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}
and
\rho^{2}_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}

are explicitly given by (
2.72
)
and (
2.74
), respectively.
This function is simply called an intensity ratio in
[
63
]
,
but here we call it an
unfolded 2-correlation function

following a terminology used in
random matrix theory
[
21
]
.

We have proved the following
(see Fig.
5
)
[
36
]
.

Theorem 2.22

(i)   When
0<q<1
, in the short distance,
the correlation is generally repulsive in common with DPPs.

(ii)   There exists a critical value

r_{0}=r_{0}(q)\in(q,1)\quad\mbox{for each $q\in(0,1)$}

such that if
r\in(r_{0},1)

positive correlation
emerges
between zeros when the distance between them
is large enough within
\mathbb{A}_{q}
.

(iii)   The limits

g_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w;r):=\lim_{q\to 0}g_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)
,

z,w\in\mathbb{D}^{\times}
and

r_{\rm c}:=\lim_{q\to 0}r_{0}(q)

are well-defined, and
r_{\rm c}
is positive.
When
r\in[0,r_{\rm c})

all positive correlations vanish in
g_{\mathbb{D}}(z,w;r)
,
while when
r\in(r_{\rm c},\infty)

positive correlations can survive.

Figure 5:

The critical curve
r=r_{0}(q)
mentioned in
Theorem
2.22
(ii)
is numerically plotted
(in red) in the fundamental cell

\Omega:=\{(q,r):q\in(0,1),q\leq r\leq 1\}

in the parameter space,
which is located between the diagonal line

r=q

and the horizontal line
r=1
.
The parabolic curve
r_{\rm c}+cq^{2}

given in
[
36
]

and the line
1-(1-q)/2

are also dotted,
which approximate
r=r_{0}(q)
well for
q\gtrsim 0
and

q\lesssim 1
, respectively.

2.6
Exercises 2

2.6.1
Exercise 2.1

(1)
By the definition of the theta function

\displaystyle\theta(z;p)

\displaystyle:=(z,p/z;p)_{\infty}=(z;p)_{\infty}(p/z;p)_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\Big\{(1-zp^{i})(1-p^{i+1}/z\Big\},

(2.95)

prove the inversion formula

\theta(1/z;p)=-\frac{1}{z}\theta(z;p)

(2.96)

and the quasi-periodicity property

\theta(pz;p)=-\frac{1}{z}\theta(z;p).

(2.97)

(2)
By direct calculation following the
definition (
2.95
), verify the equalities,

\displaystyle\theta(\zeta;p^{3})\theta(\zeta p;p^{3})\theta(\zeta p^{2};p^{3})=\theta(\zeta;p),

(2.98)

\displaystyle\theta(\zeta;p)\theta(\zeta\omega_{3};p)\theta(\zeta\omega_{3}^{2};p)=\theta(\zeta^{3};p^{3}),\quad\zeta\in\mathbb{C}^{\times},

(2.99)

where
\omega_{3}
is a primitive 3rd root of unity.

(3)
Let
x=e^{-2ia}
,
y=e^{-2ib}
,
u=e^{-2ic}
,
and
v=e^{-2id}
in
the Weierstrass addition formula

\theta(xy,x/y,uv,u/v;p)-\theta(xv,x/v,uy,u/y;p)=\frac{u}{y}\theta(yv,y/v,xu,x/u;p).

(2.100)

Consider the limit
p\to 0
and
derive the following trigonometric equality,

\displaystyle\sin(a+b)\sin(a-b)\sin(c+d)\sin(c-d)

\displaystyle\quad-\sin(a+d)\sin(a-d)\sin(c+b)\sin(c-b)

\displaystyle\qquad=\sin(b+d)\sin(b-d)\sin(a+c)\sin(a-c).

(2.101)

Show that (
2.101
) is readily verified if
we use the following addition formulas of
trigonometric functions,

\sin(a\pm b)=\sin a\cos b\pm\cos a\sin b.

(2.102)

2.6.2
Exercise 2.2

Consider the weighted Szegő kernel of

\mathbb{A}_{q}:=\{z\in\mathbb{C}:q<|z|<1\}
,

q\in(0,1)
expressed using the theta function

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;r)=\frac{q_{0}^{2}\theta(-rz\overline{w})}{\theta(-r,z\overline{w})},\quad z,w\in\mathbb{A}_{q},\quad r>0,

(2.103)

where

q_{0}:=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(1-q^{2n})=(q^{2};q^{2})_{\infty}.

(2.104)

Schottky’s theorem asserts that
the group of conformal transformations
from
\mathbb{A}_{q}
to itself
is generated by the rotations

R_{\theta}(z):=e^{\sqrt{-1}\theta}z,\quad\theta\in[0,2\pi),

(2.105)

and the
q
-inversions

T_{q}(z):=\frac{q}{z}.

(2.106)

(1)
Show that the rotational invariance is obvious.

(2)
Prove the following equality,

\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(T_{q}(z),T_{q}(w);r)=\frac{q}{r}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;q^{2}/r).

(2.107)

Note that if
r=q
the weighted Szegő kernel
is reduced to the original Szegő kernel,

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;q)
.
In this case the above becomes

\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(z)}\overline{\sqrt{T_{q}^{\prime}(w)}}S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(T_{q}(z),T_{q}(w))=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w).

(2.108)

Hence we can conclude that the
original Szegő kernel,

S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w)=S_{\mathbb{A}_{q}}(z,w;q)
,
is invariant under conformal transformations
from
\mathbb{A}_{q}
to itself.

2.6.3
Exercise 2.3

Consider the DPP of Peres and Virág,
{\cal Z}_{X_{\mathbb{D}}}
,
whose correlation kernel with respect to
m/\pi
is given by the
Bergman kernel of
\mathbb{D}
,
K_{\mathbb{D}}
;

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbb{D},\mathrm{PV}}^{n}(z_{1},\dots,z_{n})=\det_{1\leq i,j\leq n}[K_{\mathbb{D}}(z_{i},z_{j})],\quad n\in\mathbb{N},\quad z_{1},\dots,z_{n}\in\mathbb{D},
